The provisional state grants read tokens for the session's `gitClone` sources and `inject` secrets for the session's bindings, because setup commands on a snapshotless rebuild, a resume with no workspace snapshot that materializes the workspace again, use them as they did at the session's first finalize, and `inject` keeps those secrets out of the agent's reach. It serves binding definitions (hosts, credential pins, and CAs), and decisions use the session's policy, so setup commands have egress and the trust anchor (the per-pod CA certificate) is current before launch. Write tokens, `file`, `federation`, `metadata`, and any `userCredential` binding become available at the `running` commit, so a setup command that needs one of those on a rebuild fails and §6.2 sends the session to `awaiting_client_action`, the state in which it waits for its client, which the documentation states. The provisional binding lives outside the session row's `pod_assignment` field, which records the pod a session runs on. Its write is fenced on the resume attempt's coordination generation (`coordination_generation`) in §7.3's step-2 transaction, where the pod is already known, and every exit from `resuming` (the failure transition, the `resuming` watchdog's timeout, and a terminal state reached mid-resume) retires it in the same write that releases the pod, so a released pod waiting idle for its tenant (a pinned idle pod) carries no session grant. The §4.6.1 orphan-claim predicate, which finds pod claims no live session holds, keeps reading `pod_assignment`. That is a §7.3 change staged in proposal 2, and a tier 4 test resumes a session with a `gitClone` source.
- `netagent` receives the same changes through its pod binding API subscription to one replica, which is served from that replica's cache after the re-read. When the replica goes away (loss or rollout), `netagent` resubscribes to any replica, sends the last coordination generation it saw, and receives the current binding from that replica's read-through; a resubscription whose coordination generation matches the replica's cache is answered from cache, and resubscription has its own low rate limit (one per 10 seconds per pod), so a pod cannot drive store reads through it. Open tunnels stay open for a 30-second grace during resubscription and then close (P6). When a session ends or the binding changes, `netagent` closes that session's tunnels at once. On a pod with several slots it attributes each tunnel to its slot, and so to the slot's session, under "Slot attribution and fetch grants", and closes a session's tunnels when that session's slot is released. Where attribution is unavailable, as on a runtime on which neither reader answers, tunnels run to the pool's cap, which is a stated residual.

`lenny-egress-authz`, the Token Service, and `lenny-git-mirror` therefore read the session store through the pooler, with §13.2.4 rows. The Token Service reads only its own token and audit stores today (`cmd/lenny-token-service/stores.go`), so the session, pool, and binding reads are new for it. None of them holds a direct Postgres connection.

### Local egress endpoint (`netagent`)

`netagent` listens on `127.0.0.1:3128` as an HTTP CONNECT proxy that requires no client authentication. Every tool in the pod reaches the internet through it.

- The proxy variables are fixed values that are identical for every pod, so they are set at pod creation. The supervisor sets them on each runtime process it launches.
- For each CONNECT, `netagent` opens a CONNECT to `lenny-egress` carrying `Proxy-Authorization: Basic` with the service account token as the password. 
- `netagent` does not resolve the target name and does not open TLS. It forwards the CONNECT authority unchanged, and nothing else from the client: it sends `Host` and its own `Proxy-Authorization`, and it drops every other header the agent sent, so an agent cannot inject a route header, the header that selects Envoy's route for the tunnel, or an identity header. The decision overwrites the route header on every CONNECT regardless.
- `netagent` binds its loopback ports before the runtime starts and holds them. If a restart loses a port to a process the agent started, `netagent` fails its readiness probe, egress stays closed (P6), and the pod is reported unhealthy and retired. The git traffic of `lenny-materialize` (the materialization clone and the restore-completion fetch) and of `lenny-store-fill` uses the same port and the same pod trust bundle. A process standing on the port cannot pose as a covered host to the helper or to the fill, because it lacks the per-pod CA's key, and neither holds a credential to give it.
- `netagent` refuses every CONNECT and absolute-form request until it holds its first pod binding API answer after any start, including the first answer for a warm pod with no binding and the first answer after a restart. CONNECT got 503 for covered, bound, and opaque hosts before the answer and again after a restart **[measured]**.
- Before any binding exists, the pod's effective policy is the pool's warm-up policy, so the store fill and the other warm fills have egress before any tenant.
- `netagent`'s listeners set a head read deadline, 10 seconds by default, and refuse a bare LF in a request head at once. A prototype held a bare-LF request for 20 seconds with no deadline (recorded in [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`, "Bounds and cost"](user-credential-delivery-design.md#bounds-and-cost)).

### Slot attribution and fetch grants

An agent pod has one network namespace, which `netagent`, the adapter, and every slot container share ([SI-10](session-isolation-design.md#si-10-what-stays-shared)). Each slot runs under its own UID ([SI-2](session-isolation-design.md#si-2-slot-identity-and-socket-names)), and `lenny-materialize` runs in the slot under the same UID as the session's own processes ([WM-10](workspace-materialization-design.md#wm-10-git)). This section states how `netagent` tells these processes apart. [SI O9](session-isolation-design.md#open-decisions) leaves the attribution of a connection to a slot to this design, and the owner's acceptance of the materialization study on 2026-11-12 (WM decision D6) assigns the fetch grant to it, together with SI O9 (decision 52).

**Slot attribution.** When `netagent` accepts a connection on a loopback listener, it looks up the owner UID and the TCP state of the connecting socket. Its first reader is `sock_diag` (`NETLINK_SOCK_DIAG` with one `inet_diag` request keyed on the connection's addresses and ports), which an unprivileged process can query within its own network namespace. Its second reader, used where the netlink socket is refused, is `/proc/net/tcp` and `/proc/net/tcp6`, which list the same owner UID and state for every socket of the namespace. The AWS spike of 2026-11-14 measured which reader answers on each runtime, on one EC2 node with k3s 1.37, containerd 2.4.2, runc 1.5.3, gVisor `release-20261109.0`, and Kata 4.3.1 ([its report, "Test 1: per-slot attribution"](evidence/agent-pod-layout-design/attribution-and-quota-spike/RESULTS.md#test-1-per-slot-attribution)) **[measured]**:

| Runtime | `sock_diag` | `/proc/net/tcp` | `/proc/net/tcp6` |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| runc, with and without user namespaces | Answers | Answers | Answers |
| gVisor, its own network stack | Refused: `socket()` fails with `EPROTONOSUPPORT` | Answers | Answers |
| gVisor, host networking (`network = "host"`) | Refused: `EPROTONOSUPPORT` | Answers | Absent: the file does not exist |
| Kata, virtio-fs and block-plain | Refused: every request fails with `ENOENT`, because the Kata 4.3.1 guest kernel is built without `CONFIG_INET_DIAG` | Answers | Answers |

Every reader that answered gave each connection its own slot's UID, with no mismatch and no lookup error over 1,002 concurrent IPv4 connections and 1,000 IPv6 connections from three slots, and under user namespaces both readers report the UIDs inside the pod, which are the UIDs the controller renders **[measured]**. runc pools therefore attribute through `sock_diag`, and gVisor and Kata pools through `/proc/net/tcp`, so no measured runtime class is a fallback pool. The spike sets these rules **[design]**:

- **Only an established socket of a configured UID.** `netagent` accepts a reader's answer only when the socket's state is `ESTABLISHED` and its owner UID is one that `netagent` was configured with: a slot UID, the adapter UID, its own UID, or `lenny-store-fill`'s UID. It refuses every other answer, as it refuses a socket that neither reader finds. A client that closes its end before the lookup leaves a socket in `FIN_WAIT2` that both readers report with UID 0 **[measured]**, so this rule turns that false answer into a refusal. Such a connection carries no traffic, so the refusal costs nothing.
- **One read of `/proc/net/tcp` per batch of accepts.** A read of `/proc/net/tcp` parses the namespace's whole table, so its cost grows with the number of sockets: 2 to 7 ms per read with about 2,000 sockets open, and, under a burst of 1,002 accepts that each made their own read, a median accept 27 to 204 ms late on runc and gVisor and 2 to 3 ms late on Kata, with a 99th percentile up to about 0.6 s on each **[measured]**. Where `sock_diag` is refused, `netagent` resolves every accept that completed before a read started from that one read, and starts the next read for the accepts that completed during it.
- **IPv4 loopback only.** gVisor with host networking has no `/proc/net/tcp6`, so no reader there answers for an IPv6 loopback connection **[measured]**. `netagent`'s listeners bind `127.0.0.1` alone on every runtime, as the local egress endpoint already does, and none binds `::1`.
- **The start-up check.** The attribution check that `netagent` runs before its startup probe passes opens a loopback connection from its own UID to its own listener and asks each reader for that connection. A reader passes when it answers with `netagent`'s UID and the state `ESTABLISHED`, and fails when its socket is refused, when it finds no row, or when it answers with another UID or state, so a runtime that reports a closed socket's UID 0 for an established connection fails the check. `netagent` reports attribution as answering when at least one reader passes, and it uses only the readers that passed.
- **Kata's guest kernel (decision 68).** The deployer guide recommends building the Kata guest kernel with `CONFIG_INET_DIAG` and `CONFIG_INET_TCP_DIAG`, so that `sock_diag` answers in Kata's guest as it does on runc. It is a recommendation rather than a requirement: without those options Kata pools attribute through `/proc/net/tcp`, which costs more under a burst of connections.
- **Nested user namespaces and passed sockets.** A slot under gVisor or Kata can create a nested user namespace with `unshare -U`, which runc's RuntimeDefault seccomp profile refuses with `EPERM`, and both readers still report a connection made from inside it with the slot's UID **[measured]**. A socket keeps its creator's UID: a socket that one slot creates and passes to another slot over an abstract Unix socket is attributed to its creator, before or after `connect` **[measured]**, so the receiving slot acts under the creator's scope, which gives it nothing the creator could not do by relaying for it.

The controller renders the pod's slot UIDs, indexed by slot, into `netagent`'s configuration at pod creation. The gateway records each session's slot index with its pod assignment at bind, and `pkg/binding` returns, for each session of the pod, that slot index, so `netagent` maps a connection to its slot and the slot to its session. A connection whose owner is the adapter UID or `netagent`'s own UID is a platform connection under **Platform connections (decision 64).** below, one whose owner is `lenny-store-fill`'s UID is the warm fill, which `netagent` treats under the pool's warm-up policy and the warm list's authorization before READY and refuses afterwards (decision 61), and one from any other UID is refused with 403 and an `egress.local` record **[design]**. On a pool whose status records per-slot attribution, a connection whose owner neither reader finds is refused the same way. On a pool whose status records the union scope, or records no result yet, neither reader answers, so `netagent` attributes no connection, and every connection takes the pod's union scope under "Per-slot scopes" below, and the warm-up policy before READY. On a pod with one slot the lookup only refuses an unknown UID, because the slot is known. Under user namespaces on the standard class ([PM-1](placement-design.md#pm-1-node-classes-and-presets)), the lookup runs in the pod's namespace and reports the UIDs inside the pod **[measured]**. Where neither reader answers, a pod with several slots falls back to the pod-wide union scope that "Per-slot scopes" below states, and its fetch grants name the pod rather than one slot, which `sessionIsolationLevel` and `acknowledgeSharedPod`'s validation error both state. A pod with one slot needs neither reader for its scopes, because its one session is known.

**Per-slot scopes (decision 60).** On a pod with several slots, every per-session input is applied per slot: a connection attributed to a slot uses its own session's readable and writable sets, its own environment E, its own `egress.allowedHosts` and `egressLockHosts`, and its own credential bindings, and its evidence records name that session. `netagent` names the attributed session on every CONNECT it opens for such a connection, in the header `x-lenny-session`, which it strips from agent input as it strips the terminated marker; `lenny-egress-authz` accepts the header only for a session the pod's binding lists, evaluates that session's formula under "Effective policy", and, on a pool whose status records per-slot attribution, refuses a CONNECT from a pod with several slots that names no listed session and carries no platform marker. `netagent` closes a session's tunnels and flushes its cached credentials when the session's slot is released. Where attribution is unavailable on the pod's runtime class, the pod falls back to the union over its sessions for the readable and writable sets, applies no per-session credential binding, evaluates its sessions at E = ∅, and refuses `egress.allowedHosts` and `egressLockHosts` on it, as before decision 60; the session reports the fallback through `sessionIsolationLevel.perSlotScopes`, `false` on such a pod and `true` where each slot has its own scope, and `acknowledgeSharedPod`'s validation error names it. Whether attribution answers is a property of the pool's runtime class: `netagent` reports whether either reader answered before the pod reports READY, the controller records the result in the pool's status, so the result is readable at bind, and the post-chain point reads it from there ("Where the pool checks run"). A pool whose status records no result yet, because none of its pods has reported, counts as union scope at the post-chain point. A new pod whose `netagent` finds no reader on a pool whose status records that attribution answers fails its readiness and never reports READY, so no session lands on a pod whose scope differs from the one the post-chain point checked; the controller records the cause on the Sandbox, replaces the pod, and leaves the pool's status unchanged. A `netagent` whose reader stops answering refuses the connection rather than falling back on a running pod.

**Platform connections (decision 64).** `netagent` marks a connection as platform when the kernel reports the adapter UID or `netagent`'s own UID as the owner of its socket, and it marks every connection it opens itself. It sends the marker as the header `x-lenny-platform` on the CONNECT to `lenny-egress`, and `lenny-egress-authz` admits a marked CONNECT only to a platform destination: the gateway, the deployment's object storage, or the git mirror. A marked CONNECT to any other destination is refused with an `egress.decision` record, and no session's formula applies to a marked CONNECT. A slot cannot obtain the marker, because `netagent` sets it from the socket owner the kernel reports and never from the request, and it strips `x-lenny-platform` from agent input as it strips `x-lenny-session` and the terminated marker. On a fallback pod neither reader answers, so `netagent` marks only the connections it opens itself, and a connection from the adapter takes the pod's union scope as any other connection does **[design]**. The adapter's platform transports dial their endpoints directly ("Runtime SDKs", "The adapter's own transports"), so the marker governs an adapter connection that reaches `netagent` despite that.

**Fallback pools.** A pool whose status records the union scope is a fallback pool, and these rules apply to it **[design]**:

- **Status lifecycle.** The controller sets the pool's attribution status from the first start report of a pod's `netagent`, and evaluates it again when the pool's runtime class or the `netagent` image changes: it clears the status, so the pool counts as union scope at the post-chain point until a pod created after the change reports. The carrier of `netagent`'s start report to the controller and its channel register row are owed to EG-3, under the naming lint.
- **Bindings.** A fallback pod applies no per-session credential binding, so a session that names an `onRequest` binding, or whose VCS pool attaches a `poolAttach` binding, is refused at the post-chain point with `ENVIRONMENT_POLICY_NOT_ENFORCEABLE` naming the binding, rather than admitted with no credential. A binding with `scope: always` applies to every session of the tenant, and the pod is tenant-pinned, so `netagent` applies it pod-wide. A `userCredential` binding follows the pod refusals of [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md). A session that would hold an `identityAssertion` binding, under any `scope`, is refused at the post-chain point with `422 IDENTITY_ASSERTION_UNSUPPORTED_POD`, because under the union scope `netagent` cannot tell whose identity to assert (O7). Every measured runtime class records attribution, so the refusal meets only fallback pools.
- **Replacement.** A new pod whose `netagent` finds no reader on a pool whose status records per-slot attribution is replaced with the controller's pod-creation backoff, doubling up to 10 minutes, and the controller raises `NetagentAttributionUnavailable` naming the pool and the runtime class when replacements fail consecutively, because a node or runtime change can stop every new pod.
- **Fetch grants.** On a fallback pod `netagent` cannot tell the slots apart, so a grant names the pod rather than a slot: a fetch of a URL that any of the pod's open grants names, from any slot UID, is treated as materialization inside that grant's window, a grant for a second slot is accepted beside the first, and the no-widening rule holds as on any pod, because the union scope already bounds what each slot reaches.

**The fetch grant (decision 52).** A fetch by `lenny-materialize` and a fetch by the agent come from the same slot UID, so attribution alone cannot tell materialization from the session's own work. Before every helper run that may fetch (`materialize` with a `gitClone` source, `overlay` with one, and `restore-complete`), the adapter registers a fetch grant with `netagent`, and it revokes the grant when the run's `setupdone` arrives **[design]**:

- **Carrier.** The adapter dials `netagent`'s abstract Unix socket `@lenny-netagent-grant`, which `netagent` accepts only from the adapter UID by `SO_PEERCRED`. The conversation is `CH-FETCHGRANT`, an intra-pod channel with no link, JSON Lines in both directions, with the adapter as its authority. `netagent` also sends on it an `applied` message naming a slot and the coordination generation it has applied for that slot's session, which the adapter's wait before materialization, setup, and launch reads ("Delivery modes", **TLS termination for bound and covered hosts only.**), and it sends the latest one per slot again when the adapter reconnects. The channel registers of the specification gain its row through EG-3, and the naming lint has the last word on the identifier.
- **Scope.** A `grant` message names the slot, the normalized repository URLs of the run's `gitClone` sources under the shared canonicalizer ("Credentials", **The gateway runs no git.**), and a window that ends at the run's deadline, the `timeoutMs` the adapter passes on `setup` plus the supervisor's 5 s status wait (WM-11 in [`workspace-materialization-design.md`](workspace-materialization-design.md), as the asynchronous worker the owner approved on 2026-11-12 states it). `netagent` answers `granted` with the grant's identifier, or refuses a grant whose slot is not in its configuration, whose URL list passes the plan's bind bounds, or that would be a second grant for a slot that already holds one. `revoke` ends a grant, and so does its window's end, whichever comes first.
- **Treatment.** Inside the window, a fetch from the slot of a URL the grant names is materialization: the pool's fetch retry window and its hold apply on initial materialization, the 30 s wait applies on the resume path, the provisional binding's read scope applies during a resume, and the `git.operation` record names materialization as the fetch's cause. Outside a grant, a fetch is the session's own, under its binding.
- **No widening.** A grant chooses the timing rules and the record label for a fetch, and never adds to what the slot may reach. A named URL outside the session's readable set is refused as it would be without the grant, and a grant names no credential.
- **Hosts with `userAuthorization: required`.** A granted fetch of a repository under a VCS pool with `userAuthorization: required` passes the same per-user check as any fetch of the session ([UG-2](user-git-identity-design.md#ug-2-repository-authorization)): the check at bind runs before the helper starts, so a repository the user cannot read is refused before any fetch, and an entry marked `userRefused` during the window is refused inside it as outside it. `netagent` obtains the read token for a granted fetch on the pod credential API under the session's binding, with the provisional binding's read scope during a resume, and the helper holds none. The grant changes neither the token `netagent` attaches nor the checks that apply.
- **Who else is in the window.** The runtime process launches at bind, after materialization and setup ([SI-3](session-isolation-design.md#si-3-runtime-processes-slot-release-and-setup-commands)), and a helper run is the only program the worker runs during its window, so within a grant's window on initial materialization or a resume the slot runs the helper alone. A mid-session overlay runs beside the session's processes, and a process of the session that fetches a granted URL inside that window gets the same treatment, which reaches no repository the plan does not name.
- **Restarts.** A `netagent` restart drops every grant, and egress is closed while it restarts (P6). When `netagent` reports ready again, the adapter re-registers the grants of runs still in progress. An adapter restart ends its pod's runs under SI's rules, and the grants end with their windows.

The helper holds no credential: no service account token and no VCS token, because `netagent` attaches them on the git path.

**The egress halt (CR-4).** When a suspension cannot take hold, step 1 of SL-3 halts the slot's egress, so the slot's work, its requests to operator services, and the model calls it sends outside the LLM proxy stop before the pod is released rather than at the release (O9). [`connector-removal-design.md`, "CR-4 Egress halt at suspension"](connector-removal-design.md#cr-4-egress-halt-at-suspension) owns the clause in SL-3, and this design carries the mechanism in `netagent` **[design]**:

- **Trigger.** The gateway calls `HaltSessionEgress` naming the session on the adapter, and the adapter sends `halt_slot` for the session's slot to `netagent`. `netagent` closes the slot's open tunnels and in-flight requests, refuses the slot's new requests with `503`, and answers `halted`. The adapter answers the RPC once `halted` arrives. The gateway continues with the suspension's next step whether the RPC succeeds or reaches its deadline of 5 seconds, because the pod's release stops the slot's traffic in any case, and it counts the outcome in `lenny_egress_halts_total` by outcome (`halted`, `timeout`, or `unavailable`).
- **Carrier.** The conversation is `CH-EGRESSHALT`, an intra-pod channel with no link and with the adapter as its authority, as `CH-FETCHGRANT` is. The adapter dials `netagent`'s abstract Unix socket `@lenny-netagent-halt`, which `netagent` accepts only from the adapter UID by `SO_PEERCRED`, so no slot can halt or resume egress. Messages are JSON Lines in both directions. `halt_slot`, from the adapter, names a slot, and a second `halt_slot` for a halted slot is answered `halted` again. `halted`, from `netagent`, names the slot once its connections are closed. `resume_slot`, from the adapter, names a slot and ends its halt. The channel registers of the specification gain its row through CR-C of the removal design, and the naming lint has the last word on the identifier.
- **Scope.** The halt applies to the connections `netagent` attributes to the slot. Platform connections, from the adapter UID or `netagent`'s own UID, keep running, so the checkpoint and the release proceed. On a fallback pool `netagent` cannot tell the slots apart, so the adapter sends no `halt_slot`, the RPC answers that the halt is unavailable, and the slot's traffic stops at the release.
- **Lifting.** A halt ends at the slot's release, or when the gateway calls `UnsealSession` for the session on the same pod, because a session the gateway unseals on its pod is resuming there; the adapter then sends `resume_slot`. A tree suspension (SL-4) halts the egress of each session it suspends whose suspension cannot take hold. A suspension that takes hold, and a move to `dormant`, send no halt.
- **Restarts.** A `netagent` restart drops every halt, and egress is closed while it restarts (P6). When `netagent` reports ready again, the adapter reconnects and sends `halt_slot` for each slot that is still halted, as it re-registers fetch grants, so a halt lapses only between the ready report and the reconnection. An adapter restart ends its pod's sessions under the session isolation design's rules, and the halts end with them.
- **Session activity.** Egress traffic is not session activity (O8), and `netagent` reports none. A runtime whose harness waits on an operator service keeps its session active under SL-6 by reporting `busy: true` on its heartbeats, as [`connector-removal-design.md`, "CR-3 External services"](connector-removal-design.md#cr-3-external-services) states.

### Central egress proxy (`lenny-egress`)

`lenny-egress` is a Kubernetes Deployment, a replicated set of identical stateless pods, in the `lenny-system` namespace where the gateway and Token Service already run. It runs Envoy 1.39.6 or later (tested on 1.40.2 and 1.39.6), configured by a static bootstrap that the Helm chart renders. Lenny writes no proxy code.

The second spike verified each of these behaviours on both versions:

- It terminates HTTP/1.1 CONNECT and reaches the target through the dynamic forward proxy, Envoy's filter that resolves the CONNECT authority itself and dials it.
- It sends an ext_authz gRPC check to `lenny-egress-authz` for every CONNECT. The check carries the method, the authority (host and port), all request headers including `Proxy-Authorization`, and the peer address. A deny returns 403. A 407 with `Proxy-Authenticate: Basic` is available for clients that need a challenge. If `lenny-egress-authz` is unreachable, Envoy returns 503 and opens no tunnel (P6).
- It writes the decision metadata that ext_authz returns (decision identifier, tenant, pool, session, and rule) into a JSON access log, with the upstream address, the upstream source port, and bytes in each direction (the fields "Evidence streams" names), for allows and for denies. Long tunnels log at establishment and at an interval, as well as at close.
- `DnsCacheConfig.resolved_address_filter` refuses targets that resolve to private, loopback, link-local, metadata, or cluster ranges. It also covers IP literals, because literals pass through the same resolution step. The range list must include `::ffff:0:0/96` (IPv4-mapped) and `64:ff9b::/96` (NAT64). Without them, `[::ffff:127.0.0.1]` passes the filter. The list also carries the Azure wireserver `168.63.129.16/32`, `0.0.0.0/8`, `100.64.0.0/10`, `198.18.0.0/15`, multicast, and `::/128`, and refuses IPv6 zone identifiers. The list has no exemptions: a private destination a tenant is allowed to reach never passes through resolution (see "Egress decision service", **Private destinations**). The ext_authz filter runs before the dynamic forward proxy filter, so a refused authority is never resolved, which closes a DNS side channel for denied names. A conformance test fixes the list and the filter order.
- `max_stream_duration` ends active, idle, and client-only CONNECT tunnels at the configured time (5.0 seconds in the test, with `delayed_close_timeout` lowered). `max_connection_duration` does not end tunnels, and the design does not use it.
- **Tunnel lifetime.** `max_stream_duration` is route configuration in the static bootstrap, so the chart renders a fixed menu of routes (15 minutes, 1 hour, 2 hours, and 8 hours by default, as Helm values) and a pool names one of them. The decision selects that route, so the cap is a pool setting without xDS, Envoy's dynamic configuration APIs: an LLM pool can carry 2 hours while a coding pool carries 15 minutes. A pod whose `netagent` is gone keeps a tunnel until its pool's cap, attributed to the session that opened it. That is the residual of Envoy having no per-tunnel kill, and the per-pool cap bounds it.
- It chains a tunnel to an upstream proxy chosen per request. The decision response sets a route header and returns the upstream address in dynamic metadata. An `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster, an Envoy cluster that dials the address a request carries rather than resolving a configured name, then dials that address, and Envoy forwards the CONNECT with an injected `Proxy-Authorization` or identity headers. The upstream proxy received only the injected credential, and the pod's token did not reach it. Changing a tenant's upstream needs no Envoy reload.
- Throughput: four parallel 4 GB downloads completed at about 2.7 GB/s in total, with Envoy at 130% CPU and under 80 MiB of memory.

The `http_11_proxy` transport socket does not chain raw CONNECT tunnels and is not used. An `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster that reads the upstream from a request header also failed, so the upstream address always comes from ext_authz dynamic metadata.

The third round repeated the baseline on both versions and added these results:

- **ext_authz in HTTP mode** works only when Envoy reaches the decision service over HTTP/2 (h2c). Over HTTP/1.1, Envoy sends the check with method `CONNECT` and every request fails with 503. `Proxy-Authorization` reaches the check only when `allowed_headers` lists it, and the peer address needs `headers_to_add` with `%DOWNSTREAM_REMOTE_ADDRESS%`. The gRPC form needs neither and remains the default. A deny with a 5xx status in HTTP mode is treated as an authorization failure, so with Lenny's `failure_mode_allow: false` and `status_on_error: 503` Envoy 1.40.2 and 1.39.6 answer with an empty 503 and drop the decision's body and headers, while gRPC mode returns them. A proxy with `failure_mode_allow: true` would turn that deny into an allow ([`tenant-outbound-connector-design.md`, "Spikes owed"](tenant-outbound-connector-design.md#spikes-owed), Envoy spike of 2026-11-05).
- **The pod's credential on chained routes.** Unless the decision service removes it (`x-envoy-auth-headers-to-remove: proxy-authorization` in HTTP mode, or the equivalent header mutation in gRPC mode), Envoy forwards the pod's `Proxy-Authorization` to the tenant's upstream proxy. `lenny-egress-authz` removes it on every chained route, and a conformance test checks that.
- **Upstream proxy replies.** By default a chained upstream's 407 or 403 reaches the pod with its `Proxy-Authenticate` headers and body. The `custom_response` filter, matched per route, maps them to a fixed 502 or 403 with no upstream headers. `response_headers_to_remove` strips headers but passes the body, and `local_reply_config` does not apply to upstream replies.
- **IPv6.** IPv6-only targets work by literal and by AAAA-only names. `dns_lookup_family: AUTO` prefers IPv6 for dual-stack names, and `V4_PREFERRED` prefers IPv4. The address filter blocks ULA, link-local, loopback, mapped, and NAT64 addresses.
- **DNS rebinding.** The address filter runs on every resolution and refresh, and no private address was ever contacted. A name that rebinds to a private address keeps its last public address until `host_ttl` expires and is then refused. A tunnel that is already open keeps its address. Envoy flags the filter's range type as work in progress and outside its threat model at startup. The `lenny-egress` NetworkPolicy and the decision service's literal checks back it up.
- **Load** (2 CPUs, `--concurrency 2`). 1,000 concurrent tunnels had a CONNECT setup latency of 2.4 ms at p50 and 10.8 ms at p99, at 22% CPU and about 54 KiB per tunnel. 2,000 new CONNECTs per second, each with a decision call, had a p99 of 8.7 ms at 59% CPU. The default cluster circuit breaker caps each cluster at 1,024 concurrent tunnels and then queues and fails with `503 UO`, a 503 carrying Envoy's upstream overflow flag, so the chart raises the breakers on every tunnel cluster.
- **A slow decision service** adds its latency to each CONNECT. Beyond the ext_authz timeout, every CONNECT fails closed at the timeout. With the decision service stopped, CONNECTs got 503 after about 0.5 s, and service recovered about 2 s after restart. The chart sets the ext_authz timeout to 250 to 500 ms, a decision-service connect timeout of about 250 ms, a breaker on the decision-service cluster sized to the CONNECT rate times the timeout, and a global downstream connection limit.
- **Close timing.** The default 1 s `delayed_close_timeout` delays the close of a tunnel that `max_stream_duration` ends, and the chart lowers it.
- **Decision-service resilience** (round 5, 500 CONNECTs per second against two decision endpoints). With HTTP/2 keepalive (1 s interval and timeout), outlier detection on local-origin failures, an active gRPC health check that ejects in about 1 s and readmits only on a passing check, and one retry that skips the endpoint that failed (`previous_hosts`), killing one endpoint and freezing one with `SIGSTOP` each caused zero failed CONNECTs out of 30,000. Without the health check, the base configuration lost about 1% on a kill, mostly when the ejected endpoint was probed with live traffic after its ejection expired, and about 2% on a freeze. One pitfall: Envoy uses the retry policy's `max_interval` as the per-try timeout, so it must equal the per-try budget (0.4 s); at 50 ms it failed half of all CONNECTs.
- **Long streams** (round 5). 200 tunnels each carrying one chunk per second ran 20 minutes with none cut and Envoy's memory flat at about 27 to 33 MiB, with `stream_idle_timeout` 300 s, `idle_timeout` 3600 s, `max_stream_duration` 7200 s, and periodic access-log entries every 60 s. Two silent tunnels were cut at exactly 300 s by the idle timeout, as intended, and with a 120 s cap all 200 ended at 120 s with a clean close.
- **Decision latency** (round 5). Envoy has no ext_authz response cache, so caching lives in the decision service. At 1,000 CONNECTs per second with 50 ms decisions, Envoy added about 1.6 ms and held about 50 checks in flight per endpoint, so the decision service is sized at rate times latency times two.
- **Drain** (round 5). `POST /drain_listeners?graceful` with `--drain-time-s 20` kept accepting CONNECTs for 20 s and reset every tunnel between two thirds of the drain time and the drain time minus the HTTP connection manager's 5 s drain timeout, with a clean close. `/ready` stays 200 during the drain, so the Service endpoint must be withdrawn separately; a plain `SIGTERM` closes every tunnel at once. The chart's preStop hook calls the graceful drain and sleeps for the drain time, with `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` above it.

Envoy marks CONNECT alpha in a proto comment on `RouteMatch.connect_matcher` and `UpgradeConfig.connect_config` ("Note that CONNECT support is currently considered alpha in Envoy."), with a TODO to replace it with a formal tag. `upgrades.rst` marks only CONNECT-UDP and raw UDP tunnelling as alpha, and Envoy logged no alpha warning at runtime. The dynamic forward proxy, ext_authz, and `tcp_proxy` are marked stable. Envoy terminating CONNECT runs in production in Istio ambient waypoints (HBONE, Istio's tunnelling protocol over HTTP/2 CONNECT) and in Google Cloud Secure Web Proxy, which is built on an Envoy proxy pool and accepts HTTP CONNECT. Recent advisories touch CONNECT and the dynamic forward proxy, among them CVE-2025-64763 (early CONNECT data in TCP proxy mode) and CVE-2025-54588 (a use-after-free in the DNS cache). The chart therefore pins a patched Envoy release and the project tracks Envoy security advisories. A conformance suite in the Lenny test tiers covers every behaviour above.

Its own egress NetworkPolicy allows the public internet minus `egressCIDRs.excludePrivate`, cluster CIDRs, and IMDS, using the same rule as the gateway's upstream rule (§13.2.5, NET-057). It also allows the registered upstream proxies and private git services listed in Helm values, which are the only private addresses the decision can name. That policy is the backstop for the `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster, which dials any address the decision names.

### Transport security

Every pod-to-platform hop and every hop that carries a pod token uses TLS. No hop needs a pod client certificate, and the certificates come from a PKI of their own, so none of this depends on R14.

| Hop | Protection | What it protects |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Proxy hop (I2), `netagent` to the central proxy | Server TLS, verified by `netagent` against the egress CA bundle and the proxy's Service name | The service account token, or the level 1 credential |
| Git endpoint (I5), `netagent` to the mirror | Server TLS, verified the same way | The token and private repository contents |
| Pod binding API (I7), `netagent` to the decision service | Server TLS, verified the same way | The token and binding data |
| Pod credential API (I8), `netagent` to the Token Service | Server TLS, as already stated | Tokens and binding secrets |
| Egress decision API (I3) and the access log stream, Envoy to the decision service | mTLS. Envoy verifies the decision service's name, and the decision service admits only an allowlist of client SANs (`lenny-egress` and each egress group, the mirror, and the Token Service, plus the gateway and a level 2 proxy where they call the egress decision API). | The pod tokens every check carries, and the decision API itself, which a compromised mirror fetch could otherwise query with a forged pod UID to read tenant tags |

The gateway's ref resolver and the mirror's upstream fetches use the same TLS listener on `lenny-egress`. The local egress endpoint and the runtime's traffic to `netagent` stay on loopback, where a CONNECT is in plaintext and git's request inside it is TLS under the per-pod CA. A level 2 proxy must serve TLS on the proxy hop, as the level 1 contract already requires, and the hop to a chained upstream stays as the deployer's upstream defines it.

**Egress decision API operations by identity.** A flat allowlist is not enough, because a full check returns a route with an upstream address and credential headers, and a compromised mirror parent, the mirror's long-running server process that holds its credentials, could otherwise call the egress decision API directly, assert a tenant, and receive that tenant's upstream proxy credential. The decision service binds each client SAN to the operations it may call:

| Client SAN | Operations |
| :-- | :-- |
| `lenny-egress`, each egress group, and a level 2 proxy | The full check, including the route and credential headers |
| The mirror, and the gateway at level 1 | A git decision for an asserted tenant: allow or deny, and a private endpoint's address at level 1. The mirror also makes its second check on a caller's token. Neither returns credential headers. |
| The Token Service | The scope decision, with no route, and a mint-path decision for a host whose route is not `direct` |
| Egress evidence | None: `lenny-evidence` has its own API, and its allowlist binds each SAN to its streams |

The mirror's and the gateway's upstream traffic still goes through `lenny-egress`, which injects any upstream credential itself, so they never see it. The decision service honours the mirror's tenant assertion on that path only when that tenant's git route names the mirror for the host, as the Token Service's check on the pod credential API already requires, and the Token Service applies the same check before it releases a level 1 service identity to the mirror. The direct git decision on the egress decision API applies the same check. A compromised mirror parent can therefore fetch only through the routes of tenants that routed through it.

**The egress PKI.** The existing PKI is gated by `mtls.enabled` (`mtls-pki.yaml`), which a deployment using a service mesh turns off, and the Kind overlay keeps it off until R14 gives agent pods certificates. These hops therefore have their own PKI, which the chart renders whenever `netagent` is enabled, whatever `mtls.enabled` says, because the pod binding API and the pod credential API need it even with a replacement proxy and no mirror: a self-signed bootstrap, an `egress` CA, and a namespaced Issuer, as `mtls-pki.yaml` does for the other. A deployment without cert-manager supplies the Secrets by name, and the chart refuses to render otherwise. The chart renders a leaf for `lenny-egress` and one for each egress group, so each group has its own Service name, and leaves for the decision service, the mirror, the Token Service, `lenny-evidence`, the gateway, the controller, at level 1 `lenny-ops`, and later `lenny-siem-forwarder` from [`security-oversight-design.md`](security-oversight-design.md). Each leaf's SAN is its exact Service DNS name, with no wildcard. The gateway's leaf serves the ref resolver's trust and the binding push, whose listener admits only that SAN, so neither depends on `lenny-mtls-ca`. A mesh's mTLS, where present, wraps this TLS rather than replacing it. R14 may decide to merge the two PKIs if it makes the `mtls.enabled` PKI always on, and nothing in this design prevents or requires that. A deployer who enforces the `restricted` Pod Security level on `lenny-system` places the mirror in its own `lenny-mirror` namespace rather than lowering that namespace to `baseline`. That placement needs an issuer in the namespace, which proposal 5 settles.

**The CA bundle for pods.** `netagent` verifies platform hops against the egress CA bundle, which holds only the egress CA, its previous CA during a rotation, and for a replacement proxy the CA that Helm names. It never uses the TLS interception bundle (see "TLS interception"), which holds public roots, the deployer's CA, and any `tenantOverride.caBundle`, because those CAs could then impersonate a platform service. The CA's key sits in a Secret in `lenny-system`, which no agent pod can read. `lenny-ops` and the controller are inside this PKI's trust boundary, as they are for the existing one: `lenny-ops` can read Secrets in `lenny-system`, create Jobs that mount them, and patch Certificates, and the controller can create pods. Either, if compromised, can obtain the CA's key or a leaf with another component's SAN, and through it a tenant's upstream credential from the full check on the egress decision API. This is a stated residual, and [`security-oversight-design.md`](security-oversight-design.md) ([SO-7](security-oversight-design.md#so-7-f-25440-lenny-ops-rights)) owns narrowing those rights. The controller reads the public `ca.crt` from its own leaf's mount and writes a `lenny-egress-ca` ConfigMap to each agent namespace, to `lenny-system`, and to the mirror's namespace where it has its own, with a new RBAC row. Each pod mounts it into `netagent` only, without `subPath`, so kubelet updates reach warm pods, and `netagent` reads the egress CA bundle for each new connection. An endpoint registration (a git endpoint or an upstream) or a credential binding can carry its own CA, which `netagent`, the mirror, the gateway's ref resolver, and the Token Service use only to validate that endpoint or the binding's hosts, and which reaches `netagent` from the Token Service on the pod credential API. It needs no pod trust, so it works on a shared pool, and a tenant can register one for a public-address endpoint. A level 1 or level 2 proxy gets its CA and server name from `centralProxy.caBundle` and `centralProxy.serverName`, under a separate key of the ConfigMap that only the proxy hop reads, so a deployer's CA is never trusted for the git endpoint, the pod binding API, or the pod credential API.

**CA rotation.** Every component reads trust from `lenny-egress-ca` rather than from its own leaf's `ca.crt`, so a server's leaf can re-issue under a new CA without breaking a client that has not yet re-issued its own. A routine CA renewal keeps the key (`rotationPolicy: Never`), so existing chains still verify. A key rotation runs in a fixed order, and it needs a second CA Certificate and Secret, because the Issuer's `secretName` is what switches. A Helm value carries both the new and the outgoing CA, and the controller adds them to every egress CA bundle whatever its own leaf's `ca.crt` says. The rollout waits for the kubelet to deliver them, which took up to 80.8 seconds in round 10 and is the sync period rather than a bound. The Issuer then switches to the new CA. A switch re-issues nothing by itself, so every leaf is renewed, with `cmctl renew` or by waiting out its window. The value is cleared only after every re-issued leaf has been delivered to its pod. Round 10 ran this with 15 connections per second: no connection failed in 11,700 when the order waited for delivery, 817 of 6,300 failed over 55 seconds when the value was cleared as soon as the Secrets had re-issued, and a routine renewal failed none of 3,150.

Round 7 compared TLS with plaintext on one harness, on Envoy 1.40.2 and 1.39.6 for the feature matrix. Allow, deny, forwarded chaining with an injected credential, the server-name check (that a tunnel's TLS server name matches its CONNECT authority, under "Residual risks and limits", **SNI and domain fronting**) through the internal listener, every SSRF case, IPv6, and fail-closed behaviour gave the same outcomes. So did the raw tunnel, the tunnel lifetime cap, and a drain with 100 open tunnels. With decision endpoints killed and frozen at 500 CONNECTs per second, 0 of 30,000 failed with TLS on the proxy hop, and 0 of 30,000 with TLS on the proxy hop and mTLS on the egress decision API. The decision service refused a client outside the allowlist and one with no certificate, and Envoy failed closed against an untrusted or a plaintext decision service. The stand-in for the mirror served a full clone of 422,967 objects in 22 s with and without TLS, with identical shallow, partial, deepen, and progress behaviour.

The chart and the design carry these rules from that round:

- **CPU limits on `netagent`.** TLS costs `netagent` the kernel's zero-copy path, moving its CPU from 0.16 to 0.81 CPU-seconds per GiB. Under a 50m hard CPU limit, throughput fell from 329 to 34 MB/s, under 250m from 610 to 497 MB/s, and at 1 CPU or with no limit it did not change. `netagent` therefore has resources of its own, counted within the pool's resource class as the class mapping states: a CPU and memory request from the chart, 100m CPU and 128 MiB by default, a memory limit of 512 MiB by default, and no CPU limit of its own, so the namespace LimitRange's default of 2 CPUs applies (decision 66). The chart refuses a `netagent` limit below 250m, in its own value and in a per-namespace LimitRange override.
- **Certificate reload.** Envoy loads its certificate through SDS on a watched directory, which needs `node.id` and `node.cluster` in the bootstrap. A symlink swap in the Secret-mount form rotated the certificate with 200 tunnels open and none closed. Invalid material kept the last good certificate.
- **Readiness needs a handshake.** Envoy reports `LIVE` on `/ready` while refusing every connection in each of these states at start: a missing directory, an empty mount, missing keys, and invalid material. With a missing directory it does not recover when the certificate appears. Once Envoy's client certificate for the egress decision API also comes through SDS, invalid material at start makes Envoy exit and recover through back-off restarts, and the kubelet keeps the pod not Ready either way. The chart therefore adds a small TLS listener that shares the SDS secret and answers `/healthz` with 200, and the readiness probe uses it with `scheme: HTTPS`, which skips verification but requires a handshake. Round 7 confirmed that listener fails in all four states and passes with a valid certificate, and round 10 confirmed it with the kubelet's own probe against a real Secret, with recovery in 16 to 38 seconds once a valid Secret appeared. No pod-side rule reaches it. The chart also makes the Secret mount non-optional, so an absent Secret keeps the container from starting. The Go components fail at start on invalid material and keep their last good certificate on a failed reload.
- **Expiry is an outage.** Clients refuse an expired, wrong-name, or untrusted certificate, and they refuse TLS 1.1, so every failure fails closed, and an expired certificate stops all egress through that replica. The readiness probe skips verification, so it passes an expired leaf, and Envoy's day-granular expiry stat reads 0 on a healthy 24-hour leaf, so neither can drive the alert. The chart extends `CertExpiryImminent` (`lenny_cert_expiry_seconds`) to the new Certificates, and a rejected reload raises an alert on `sds.server.key_rotation_failed`. `lenny-ctl doctor`'s `certManagerExpiring` remedy deletes the Secret of any Certificate it matches, which includes every 24-hour leaf, and a CA's Secret holds its key. The egress Certificates therefore carry `lenny.dev/doctor-optout`, a hard skip, until [SO-6](security-oversight-design.md#so-6-f-2569-certificate-windows-and-the-remedy) in [`security-oversight-design.md`](security-oversight-design.md) fixes the remedy.
- **CA rotation.** An egress CA bundle holding the old and new CAs verified a server under either, and round 10 ran the whole order below.
- **Reload on the egress decision API.** Envoy reloads its client certificate for the egress decision API through SDS, and a new certificate reaches the peer only on a new connection, so the cluster for that API sets a `max_connection_duration` below the leaf's renewal window. The decision service checks its SAN allowlist in Go's `VerifyConnection`, because `VerifyPeerCertificate` does not run on resumed handshakes, which were 41 of 44 in round 10.
- **Sizing.** CONNECT setup at p50 did not change. The p99 went from 5.4 to 13 ms at 1,000 CONNECTs per second and from 9.7 to between 21 and 47 ms at 2,000. Envoy's CPU at 2,000 per second went from 60% to about 82% of one core, its bulk cost from 0.59 to 0.87 CPU-seconds per GiB, and its memory by about 9 KiB per tunnel. Bulk throughput did not change, at about 1.3 GB/s in total, where the client side was the limit. TLS session resumption worked (398 of 400 handshakes). On the pod binding API, 2,000 streams used 136 MiB against 76 MiB, about 30 KiB more per stream, and after a server restart all 2,000 returned in 2 s against 3 s at 1 CPU, and in 9 s against 5 s at 0.25 CPU, with no failed stream. mTLS on the egress decision API added no measurable latency: with no faults the p99 at 500 per second was 2.8 ms against 10.7 ms with that API in plaintext, and under faults 3.1 ms, with the worst case at about 420 ms either way. One earlier run under faults showed 166 ms, so these tail figures vary between runs by more than the cost of TLS.

### Default proxy choice

| Candidate                     | Result in the hands-on spike                                                                                                                                                                                | Lenny code                                     | Main trade-off                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| :---------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Envoy 1.40                    | Passed CONNECT, per-connection decisions, SSRF filtering, the lifetime cap, and per-tenant chaining                                                                                                         | None (configuration only)                      | No per-tunnel kill. `netagent` closes tunnels in the pod, and `max_stream_duration` bounds a misbehaving pod.                                                                                             |
| Go proxy on Smokescreen       | Passed every test, including the lifetime cap and kill by lease within a second                                                                                                                             | About 200 lines in the spike, plus hardening   | Lenny owns a security-critical proxy and tracks a library with no tagged releases. The spike found two pitfalls Lenny code must close: a client header that selects an upstream, and a 407 on every deny. |
| Squid 7.7 with a Lenny helper | Passed everything except the lifetime cap: `client_lifetime` did not end active tunnels                                                                                                                     | A helper of about 30 lines, plus configuration | Revocation waits for the helper cache TTL. Per-tenant upstream credentials are static configuration lines. Squid has a heavy CVE history.                                                                 |
| Others                        | HAProxy, Caddy forwardproxy, nginx with the CONNECT module, Pomerium, Tinyproxy, mitmproxy, Apache Traffic Server, and the agent sandbox proxies each failed one or more requirements, or were unmaintained | n/a                                            | Ruled out. agentgateway 1.7 merits a later look.                                                                                                                                                          |

Envoy is the recommended default because it met every requirement with configuration alone, it implements the decision API natively over a widely used wire protocol (ext_authz, also used by Istio's CUSTOM authorization), and deployers who replace the default at level 2 can use the same protocol. The missing per-tunnel kill is covered by `netagent` and the lifetime cap. The Smokescreen-based proxy is the fallback if Envoy's CONNECT support regresses.

### Egress decision service (`lenny-egress-authz`)

`lenny-egress-authz` is a Lenny-built Deployment that serves the egress decision API (I3) and the pod binding API (I7). Credentials come from the Token Service on the pod credential API (I8), with the decision service running one of the mint checks.

- It validates the caller's service account token and resolves the pod's binding.
- It computes the effective policy. The rules are under "Effective policy".
- It returns allow or deny, the route (`direct` or a named upstream with its address and credential or identity headers), the route's tunnel cap, and the attribution tags.
- It enforces quotas (P7). Rates (new CONNECTs and decision calls per second, per pod and per tenant) are counted in the decision service, which sees every open. Concurrency needs a close signal that ext_authz lacks, so `netagent` enforces the per-pod concurrent-tunnel quota in the pod, and Envoy streams its access log to the decision service through its gRPC access log service, which gives the per-tenant concurrency count and the tunnel-close visibility the audit trail needs. That stream is lossy: Envoy buffers and drops when the stream is down or full, and a restarting Envoy emits no close for its open tunnels. The periodic long-tunnel log entries therefore serve as heartbeats; a counted tunnel with no record for three intervals expires; the count resets per Envoy instance when the stream's identity changes; and the per-tenant concurrency quota is approximate, with an alert when the count and the per-pod sums disagree. The per-pod limit in `netagent` is the exact bound. Unbound warm pods count against a per-pool bucket. The chart refuses a configuration whose tenant quotas sum to more than Envoy's global downstream limit and tunnel breakers, and the defaults leave room for warm-up parallelism (`npm ci`) and for `maxConcurrentSessionsPerPod` times the LLM streams a session sends outside the gateway's LLM proxy. The mirror caps `upload-pack` concurrency per tenant.
- It rejects IP-literal targets in non-canonical forms (for example `0177.0.0.1` or `2130706433`) and private literals, because the proxy and the decision service can interpret those forms differently.
- It records every decision for audit (P5), in the `egress.decision` stream (see "Evidence streams").
- It caches decisions per pod and host for a short TTL (30 seconds by default). The cache holds the policy outcome, and a route target's kind can resolve its address and credential per call, because a cached connector grant or replica would be stale. The cache key includes the terminated marker, so an unmarked CONNECT to a credential-pinned host cannot reuse a marked allow, and, on a pod with several slots, the session `x-lenny-session` names, so one slot's allow never serves another slot's session (decision 60), and a policy version that every tenant policy, host set, route, overlay, and binding write increments, so an edit takes effect at the next decision rather than at TTL expiry, and a pod's entries are dropped when its binding changes.
- It compares an asserted route, which stops a route edit from sending a user's token or a client secret somewhere other than the route that token was stored under. The token trust record of [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md) stores the route a token may take, the caller asserts the route it compared, and the decision service refuses the connection when its own first matching route differs. The comparison applies on a call that carries a user token or a client secret: from `netagent` after the Token Service releases a `userCredential` binding's token to it, or under `allowRuntimeHeldCredentials`, and from the Token Service on a call to a link provider's endpoints. A mint of a GitHub App installation token or a GitLab project access token carries neither, and it keeps the decision it has today, on the path the fetch takes, with no route assertion and no refusal for lacking one. The caller sends, in a header on the CONNECT as the terminated marker is sent, the list it matched (`egress` for the tenant's egress routes, `git` for its git routes, or `default` for the platform default route) and a digest of the route from the token trust record it compared. A request's class, git path or API, tries its own list, then the other list, then the platform default, in the order [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md) writes out, in both the assertion and the decision, so a host with only a git route, as in "A self-hosted GitLab on a private address", and a host with only an egress route, as on an outbound connector, both work **[design]**. The decision service's git list omits a git route to `direct` or the mirror, as the record does. It refuses when the first match in the named list differs, including on a cached decision, so a route edit reaching it first stops the token rather than redirecting it. The cache key includes the asserted list, because a host with both lists has two routes, and an unasserted CONNECT's key does not change. A level 2 proxy that drops the header gets no comparison, and its conformance row is the control. A link provider in its `deleting` state, while it drains its stored tokens, or one suspended by a guardrail, still declares its endpoints for revocation. `netagent` strips this header from agent input, and at level 1 its decision call on the pod binding API carries the same assertion. `netagent` keys its upstream pool on the list and the route digest, and a route write closes pooled upstream connections to the hosts it changes **[design]**, because idle pooled connections carried git past a route edit in a run, on a stand-in with no per-client-CONNECT decision **[measured]**. [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md) defines the record.
- It answers the Token Service's scope checks (see **Credential requests** below). It never holds a pod's Token Service token and never requests a credential itself, so a compromised decision service can misdecide, but it cannot mint. With [OC-1](tenant-outbound-connector-design.md#proposals), the outbound connector of [`tenant-outbound-connector-design.md`](tenant-outbound-connector-design.md), it also holds a grant signing key, and that design states the bound.

**Credential requests.** `netagent` requests upstream git tokens, binding secrets, federation JWTs, and `metadata` credentials from the Token Service directly, on the pod credential API, presenting its `lenny-token-service` audience token. The Token Service and the decision service each check every mint, independently and from their own reads. These are the mint checks:

- The Token Service verifies the pod token offline against the cluster issuer and resolves the pod itself through the shared `pkg/binding` package, which both components run as one implementation with two independent instances. The package is Kubernetes-backed for identity: an informer over the agent namespaces and `lenny-system` (the latter for the mirror and gateway service identities) gives the pod UID, the `lenny.dev/tenant-id` label (the authoritative tenant pin, `tenantpin.go`), the Sandbox and its pool, and `status.podIPs` for the peer-address check. The informer stores a projection of each pod (UID, name, namespace, labels, `podIPs`, and phase) rather than whole Pod objects, so three components caching the agent pods at the §17 scale stay small. Before the informer has synced, every pod is unknown and every request fails closed; on API server loss client-go serves its last cache, so a deleted pod's token stays valid until its expiry (600 s), which is the stated bound. The same outage stops the kubelet from renewing projected tokens, so every pod's egress fails closed about 600 s into it, the Kubernetes minimum for `expirationSeconds`; that is a stated residual with an alert, and it bounds how long existing pods keep working during an API server outage, and the component reports the stale cache in its readiness detail without failing the Token Service's readiness for the gateway lease path, the gateway's own calls that lease VCS credentials to sessions, the only leases left once LU-4 removes LLM credential leases (F-4.3.3). It reads the pool record (owner tenant and overlay) and the session binding, including the provisional one, from the stores through the pooler, and the binding definitions (the VCS pool, the credential binding with its `hosts` and `attach` template, and the session's writable set) from the same stores. The pod state table (`agent_pod_state`) is not an identity source: its `tenant_id` is a convenience column set on claim, as its schema comment says. The Token Service therefore gains `list` and `watch` on pods in the agent namespaces and `lenny-system`, and a pooler read of the pool and session stores (§13.2.4), and it enforces the writable set, the tenant allowlist, and the peer check from its own reads.
- It then asks `lenny-egress-authz` on the egress decision API for the scope decision. That call carries the verified pod UID, the observed peer address, and the requested scope, and never the pod's token, so a compromised decision service collects nothing it can replay. The decision returns allow or deny and the attribution tags, and no secret; tenant identity never comes from the egress decision API in either component. The mint happens only when both mint checks agree, and each has derived the tenant from Kubernetes on its own.
- The pod credential API response carries the binding's authoritative `hosts` and `attach` template, and any resolved `requestRules`, from the Token Service's own read, and `netagent` limits injection, and TLS termination for those hosts, to that set. The copy of the bindings in the pod binding API subscription is informational, so a compromised decision service cannot widen the hosts a secret travels to, and cannot widen a write mint within a tenant either. It can misdecide a CONNECT, which is the bound of a compromised policy decision point. The one input the pod binding API decides is the covered set, because a warm or public-only pod makes no pod credential API call. A compromised decision service can remove a host from that set, and it also evaluates the terminated marker, so nothing stops it there: git to that host then goes as an opaque tunnel, a token the agent holds reaches it, and git enforcement is off for that pod. No mint travels on that path, because `netagent` asks the pod credential API only on the git path. This is the same bound, and credential pins arrive on the pod credential API, so a credential-pinned host stays terminated.

- The gateway stamps `lenny.dev/tenant-id` at bind, so a component's informer can see the label after the pod's first request arrives. `pkg/binding` therefore reports a pod whose session binding exists and whose label has not yet appeared as not yet visible, which it keeps distinct from an unknown pod. Every `pkg/binding` instance (the Token Service, the decision service, and the mirror) answers a retryable error for that state, `netagent` retries with backoff for up to 5 seconds, and nothing is minted or allowed until the label is seen. A label that never appears fails closed when the retry bound ends.

`netagent` caches each minted credential until the earlier of its `expiresAt` and the pod credential API response's `cacheUntil`, and refreshes it shortly before, so a binding under `npm ci` or `gh` costs one pod credential API call per lifetime rather than one per request. The cache is keyed on the pod binding's coordination generation, the session (the tenant pin for bindings with `scope: always`), and the binding definition's version, and `netagent` flushes a session's entries on every binding change on the pod binding API, at the session's end, and at the release of the session's slot ([SI-3](session-isolation-design.md#si-3-runtime-processes-slot-release-and-setup-commands)), which the gateway also pushes as a binding change. On a pod with several slots the entries of one slot's session serve only connections attributed to that slot (decision 60). A tenant admin's edit of a binding definition (a host removed, a binding deleted) bumps that version, and the gateway pushes it as a binding change, so the edit reaches the pod at once rather than at the credential's expiry, so a credential never outlives the session it was minted for on a recycled pod or a pod with several slots. A stored secret has no provider expiry, so the Token Service assigns it a cache lifetime of 15 minutes, as its `cacheUntil`, which bounds how long a rotation takes to reach a pod. An absent `cacheUntil` means `expiresAt`. A pod credential API response also carries `handling`, a list of names with an optional minimum version, and `netagent` refuses a credential naming anything it does not implement at that version, so an older image cannot attach a credential without a required step. `handling` is empty until a proposal in another design sets it. The direct path keeps the pod's Token Service token away from the decision service. A decision service that relayed that token would hold a valid token for every active pod, and a compromised one could replay each for anything the pod's binding allowed.

**The pod credential API listener.** The pod credential API (I8) is the first pod-reachable surface of the component that holds every provider credential, and today the Token Service admits only the gateway over mTLS (§13.2.4). The pod credential API therefore runs on its own port and listener with its own connection and goroutine budget, so pod load cannot starve the gateway-facing lease path or trip the gateway's Token Service breaker (F-4.3.3); it caps connections and requests per source address before authentication, and per pod and per tenant after it; it serves TLS under the egress CA, which `netagent` verifies (see "Transport security"), so a stored push token or an API key never crosses the cluster network in cleartext; and P7 counts credential calls with the other quotas. The NetworkPolicies for pools with `egressProfile: proxied`, for pools with `egressProfile: restricted` and `gitAccess`, and for pools with `egressProfile: internet` that run `netagent` gain the pod credential API port as a pod-side peer, and the `lenny-system` ingress rule for the Token Service gains the agent namespaces on that port only. The mirror's own fetches use the same listener on the mirror's mTLS identity, scoped to the store's tenant and upstream and naming the pool and the cause of the fetch.

**When the Token Service is unavailable**, `netagent` fails closed: a request to a bound host with no valid cached credential gets 503 from `netagent`, a federation token that cannot be rewritten before expiry is removed at expiry, and a `metadata` fetch returns 503. Cached credentials keep working until they expire, which bounds the outage a session notices to the credential lifetimes. The other consumers fail closed the same way: the gateway's `ls-refs` for a private repository returns `GIT_CLONE_REF_RESOLVE_TRANSIENT`, the adapter's materialization clone retries within its timeout, and a tenant-owned pool's warm fill retries with backoff while the pod stays out of READY, as 0111 requires, which the pool capacity alert surfaces.
- It receives `netagent`'s local decisions over the pod binding API (path enforcement refusals, redirect refusals, scrub hits, and tunnels closed at a binding change) and records them with the central ones, each with a decision identifier that the refusal returned to the agent carries. `lenny-ctl` queries decisions by session, so a 403 is traceable whichever hop produced it.
- It serves the egress decision API on a listener that the `lenny-system` NetworkPolicy admits only from `lenny-egress`, mirror, and Token Service pods, and from gateway pods at level 1, the pod binding API on a separate port with a per-pod rate limit, so a pod cannot flood the decision path, and the binding push on a port admitted only from gateway pods.

**Port scoping.** Every pod-side egress rule names a port: the proxy port on `lenny-egress`, the pod binding API port on `lenny-egress-authz`, the minting port on the Token Service, the git port on the mirror, and 53 on the `proxied` DNS resolver, as the chart already does for every existing peer. Envoy's admin interface, whose endpoints (`/quitquitquit`, `/drain_listeners`, `/runtime_modify`, `/config_dump`) authenticate nothing, binds to loopback inside the `lenny-egress` pod, and its metrics are served on a separate listener that the `lenny-system` policy admits only from the scrape source. The access log service port on `lenny-egress-authz` admits only `lenny-egress`. A tier 9 test confirms that a pod cannot open any other port on any of these pods.

**Private destinations.** A destination on a private address (a corporate gateway, an internal GitLab, or any platform-registered private service) is never resolved by Envoy. The registration stores the address, the grant names the tenants, and the decision returns the address in dynamic metadata for a granted tenant, so an `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster dials it. A private origin (a GitLab or a CRM) is reached by terminating the CONNECT and tunnelling raw bytes to that cluster, whereas a private upstream proxy receives a forwarded CONNECT; the spike validated the forwarded form, and round 6 validated the raw-tunnel form, including stock git through a pinned address. At the default level and level 2 a pod therefore has no network path of its own to a private destination, so a raw socket from any pod is dropped, and the `ipBlock` entries belong to the central proxy alone. A level 2 proxy must dial the address the decision returns and never resolve the name itself. `ORIGINAL_DST` gives Envoy this behaviour, and `centralProxy.level2.pinnedDestinations` defaults to `false`, so a deployer asserts this capability, and validators refuse private destinations until it is `true`. A tenant route may name only upstreams, endpoints, and groups the tenant holds, and the decision refuses any other. A compromised decision service can steer a tenant to any registered `ipBlock`; that residual is bounded by the `lenny-egress` NetworkPolicy, which admits only the registered addresses. The address filter keeps every private range with no exemption, which closes the path where a tenant points a hostname it controls at another tenant's private service. A tenant-registered public service (an upstream proxy or a git endpoint) is resolved at registration, its addresses are stored, the registration is refused when any address falls in the deployment's full exclusion list, in a registered `ipBlock`, or in the cluster's own node and load-balancer addresses (a hairpin to the cluster's public address is public by range), and the decision service re-resolves it periodically and suspends the registration when a later answer fails the same check. Private registrations stay with the platform admin, because each needs an `ipBlock` in the `lenny-egress` NetworkPolicy.

### Evidence streams

The egress and git path produces records that three audiences use: the tenant admin debugging a refusal, the customer's security team, and the operator's forensics team. These records are specified here, because their format is costly to change later and this design is their first producer. Other designs consume them (see "Reserved for other designs").

**Model.** Evidence is a set of typed streams with one common envelope. A new kind of evidence is a new stream, with no change to any existing stream, to the store, or to export. Lenny does not extend an existing stream's payload with a different kind of evidence, because streams differ in volume, retention, sensitivity, and trust.

| Envelope field | Meaning |
| :-- | :-- |
| `stream` and `schemaVersion` | The stream's name and its payload version. Payloads change by adding fields or a new version. |
| `producer`, `incarnation`, and `sequence` | The verified producing service, taken from its SAN, a value that changes when an instance restarts and that also separates a service's replicas, and a sequence number per producer, incarnation, stream, and tenant, so a consumer of one tenant's records can detect a gap. An incarnation with no closing record may have lost its unflushed records. A producer that cannot number its own records, which is Envoy, has them numbered at ingest, and `incarnation` then names the writing replica's incarnation. |
| `observedAt` | When the producer observed the event. |
| `tenantId`, `poolId`, `podUid`, `sessionId`, `environment`, `crossedFromEnvironment` | Attribution, where the event has it, set from the verified binding and session row and never from a producer's claim. A warm pod's records have no tenant and sit under a platform prefix. A quarantined pod's records, from the moment the `quarantined` state applies, also sit under the platform prefix with the tenant in the payload, so a tenant's reads do not reveal a quarantine and an investigator can still attribute each record, as **Records after a quarantine** under [FE-6](forensics-evidence-preservation-design.md#fe-6-agent-activity-streams) states. |
| `traceId` | The session's trace identifier, which joins records across streams and to tool calls. |
| `provenance` | `enforced` for a record from a component outside the pod that the action passed through, `observed` for one from a component outside the pod that watched without being in the path, and `self_reported` for one from inside a pod or from a component outside the platform's control, such as a customer's connector agent. A consumer treats a `self_reported` record as evidence only while its producer is uncompromised. |
| `payloadKeyRef` | Absent on these streams. On a later stream with sensitive content, it names a key for a session or a tenant and pod incarnation that encrypts the payload (see **Erasure and holds**). |
| `sequenceEnd` | Optional. A record can carry a run of records as one sequence range: a chunk of one session's payloads, because a payload encrypted per record does not compress in a batch (about 226 bytes per payload against about 40 in round 11), or an aggregate of repeated denials. A range covers contiguous sequence numbers only. On a stream encrypted per session the sequence is per session and the position includes the session, because two sessions interleaved in one tenant's sequence overlap, and one session's range would hide the other's lost chunk. |
| `payload` | The typed body. |

**Streams in this design:**

| Stream | Producer | Provenance | Payload |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `egress.decision` | The decision service | `enforced`, and `self_reported` at level 1, where no action passes through it | Decision identifier, authority, port, peer address, outcome, rule, route target, egress group, and any shadow policy's outcome |
| `egress.tunnel` | `lenny-egress` (Envoy), through the access log stream to the decision service | `enforced` | Decision identifier, upstream address, upstream source port, start, end, bytes in each direction, and how it ended |
| `egress.accesslog` | `lenny-egress` (Envoy), directly to `lenny-evidence` under the `lenny-egress` service identity, which the ingest allowlist binds to this stream | `enforced` | The fields Envoy itself measures, with the decision identifier as a lookup key, so a reconciler has a source a compromised decision service does not control |
