The mirror's upstream call uses the gateway's 1.5-second CONNECT budget, so an Envoy outage reaches the store fallback quickly, and it has an 8-second limit overall, below the gateway's 10-second cap, and it coalesces identical `ls-refs` calls per store and ref for 5 seconds, so one tenant's volume does not multiply against a host that throttles by source address. It answers from the store only for an explicit list of causes: no reply from `lenny-egress`, a CONNECT 503, a failed connection or TLS handshake to the host, a timeout, or a host 502, 503, or 504 inside the tunnel. Every other answer is passed on and never falls back: a host 401, 403, 404, or 429, so a repository made private does not resolve from a public store, and a CONNECT 403 or 502, because `custom_response` turns a chained gateway's refusal into a 502. An unavailable mint counts as a listed cause when the provider or the Token Service is down, and a refused mint does not. The fallback also requires a confirmation within the last 6 hours (a Helm value). A confirmation is a (ref, SHA) pair recorded by a successful `ls-refs` or fetch, including a prefetch or a warm fill, and the store index persists it across a mirror restart. A successful answer whose `ref-prefix` covers a ref and omits it clears that ref's pair, so a deleted ref does not resolve during an outage, and other refs' pairs are untouched. The fallback answers only with the ref's most recent pair, and only when the store holds that commit, so a force-push seen by `ls-refs` just before a host failure returns the transient error rather than the retracted commit. A host 401, 403, or 404 and a refused mint stop the store's fallback and clear its confirmations until the next success, so a later 503 cannot serve a repository made private or removed from an App installation. The session records `resolvedFromStore` and the confirmation time, which `GET /v1/sessions/{id}` reports, so an old commit is visible and its age is bounded. Beyond that age the answer is `GIT_CLONE_REF_RESOLVE_TRANSIENT`. The mirror still asks the egress decision API on every request, so the fallback covers upstream and `lenny-egress` outages only, and a decision service outage returns the transient error. When the answer names a commit the store lacks, the mirror schedules a fetch, so the first session for a new repository is not held by a full fetch; the pod's later fetch of an object the store lacks waits on that scheduled fetch: the mirror reads the `want` lines before it runs `upload-pack` and answers 503 with a retry hint when the covering fetch is still running. A `want` the store lacks with no covering fetch, which a plan that pins a full SHA produces because it skips resolution, schedules a fetch of that repository under the caller's binding and gets the same answer; a fetch that completes without the object ends the wait with a failure. An agent can trigger these misses by looping `git fetch origin <random-sha>`, so scheduling is bounded by a 60-second negative cache per store and SHA, which any completed fetch of the repository clears so a plan pinned to a newly pushed commit is not refused, a per-tenant rate on scheduled fetches (P7), and a minimum interval between fetches of one repository, which keeps one tenant from delaying a public store or spending the mirror's shared source address. git does not retry, so `netagent` holds the pod's request and retries the mirror with backoff for the pool's fetch retry window before it returns the failure to git. The window applies once per initial materialization, over every `gitClone` source of the plan together, and it must sit below the watchdog that bounds it: `maxFinalizingTimeoutSeconds` (600 s by default, §6.2) less the Runtime's setup bound (`setupPolicy.timeoutSeconds`, 300 s by default), less 60 s, which admits the 180 s default. The pool validator reads the Runtime for that bound, taking the derived-runtime maximum where one applies (§5.1) and the §6.2 default for a pool without a Runtime reference. A write to a base Runtime that raises the setup bound is refused while any pool whose window reads that bound no longer fits it, whether the pool is a platform pool or a tenant-owned pool of a runtime derived from that base under [`tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`](tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md). The refusal names those pools and the smallest `gateway.maxFinalizingTimeoutSeconds` at which every window would fit, so the platform admin can lower a window or raise the finalize bound first. This is an exception to that design's rule that a base write always succeeds (its DP9), set by the owner on 2026-11-09. A template write that raises the window is refused the same way when a pool copied from the template would no longer fit, and the refusal names the same values. A tenant's own write that raises its derived runtime's setup bound past what its owned pool's window admits is refused, and the refusal goes to the tenant. A snapshotless resume rebuild runs the clone and the setup commands under the 300 s `resuming` watchdog (§7.3), which leaves no room for the window, so on the resume path `netagent` waits 30 s at most and then fails fast; a resumed session's repositories were fetched when the session first ran, so a pending fetch there means the mirror lost its store, and the next resume attempt succeeds once the fetch completes. The mirror's fetch continues after the pod gives up, so a repository too large for the window fails the first session at finalize as `SESSION_CREATION_FAILED` with the reason `git_fetch_pending` (§6.2 surfaces materialization failures there, and the `GIT_CLONE_REF_*` codes keep their plan-bind meaning) and serves the next one from the store; the gateway's `ls-refs` miss at plan bind already starts that fetch before the pod is claimed, which covers most repositories. For a large repository a tenant admin prefetches it through the mirror prefetch resource of the admin API, or lists it as a `gitRepository` warm fill, so no session pays for its first fetch. The prefetch resource is a tenant admin's alone. A prefetch request names a repository and may name the pool it falls under, and every prefetch record carries a pool: when the request omits the pool and exactly one pool covers the repository, the API stores that pool, and when several pools cover it, the API refuses a request that omits the pool. The mirror fetches a prefetch into the tenant's store, or into the public store for an anonymous fetch. An upstream outage therefore blocks session creation only for refs the upstream has not confirmed within the age limit. The SHA then travels to the pod in the plan, and the helper clones that SHA. No SHA reported from the pod is trusted, and nothing the helper reports is trusted beyond the session's own outcome ([WM-5](workspace-materialization-design.md#wm-5-result-validation-and-what-a-forged-result-can-do)).
- Resolution depends on the egress path at session create, and round 6 ran that path through Envoy against real hosts. The gateway mounts a projected token with the `lenny-egress` audience and has no fallback that dials a host directly.
  - **One tunnel per call.** The client disables connection reuse. With reuse, 20 calls alternating between two tenants produced one decision, attributed to the first tenant, so the second tenant's policy and routes were never consulted.
  - **Attempts.** Each attempt must receive its CONNECT reply within 1.5 seconds, and a failed attempt retries once on a new connection, inside the 10-second cap. A stopped or frozen Envoy replica under load then caused no failed call, at a cost of 1.5 seconds at worst.
  - **Errors.** No CONNECT reply, a proxy 503, 502, or 429, the time cap, and a host 5xx or 429 map to `GIT_CLONE_REF_RESOLVE_TRANSIENT`, and a proxy 502 also raises an alert because it can mean a chained gateway refused Lenny's credential. A CONNECT 403 or 407 maps to `GIT_CLONE_REF_UNRESOLVABLE` with the reason `egress_denied`, and a host 401, 403, or 404, the byte cap, a malformed response, a refused redirect, and an absent ref map to `GIT_CLONE_REF_UNRESOLVABLE`. A host 401 or 403 carries the reason `auth_failed`, and a 404 carries `not_found`, because hosts answer 404 for a private repository the caller cannot see. The CONNECT reply decides the class, because a dial timeout in Go also satisfies `context.DeadlineExceeded` and would otherwise read as a slow host.
  - **Private git endpoints.** The ref resolver reaches a platform-registered private endpoint the way pods do, as a raw tunnel through `lenny-egress` to the address the decision pins, so it shares their availability. Level 1 differs, as "Level 1 replacement" states.
  - **Scope of the dependency.** Sessions without `gitClone`, and plans that pin a full commit SHA, need no resolution and are unaffected. During a `lenny-egress` outage, callers get a retryable error within 3 seconds, and a mirror-routed repository resolves from the store within its age limit. During a decision service outage every resolution returns the retryable error. Pods that clone through `lenny-egress` fail closed in the same outage (P6), so for them the dependency adds no outage that a directly dialing ref resolver would have avoided. A pod that clones from the mirror can still clone during a `lenny-egress` outage, and its session can start when the store resolves the ref.
  - **Cost.** Envoy added about 0.3 ms per call against a local host, and the hosts' own latency (280 to 390 ms at p50) dominated against real ones. The path served 4,271 calls per second with a p99 of 130 ms on a laptop, far above any session create rate.
  - **Deployments without a central proxy.** A plan that needs resolution fails with `GIT_CLONE_REF_UNRESOLVABLE` and the reason `no_egress_path`. Level 2 proxies must pass `x-lenny-tenant`, `x-lenny-session`, the terminated marker, and the asserted route to their egress decision API call, and a conformance row tests each. Level 1 follows "Level 1 replacement".
- F-14.1.37 is closed by deleting the gateway clone path rather than by adding git to the gateway image. The clone runs the pinned static git that a Lenny release builds and the stage init container copies into `lenny-bin` with mode `0111` (WM decision D3), so neither the adapter image, which is distroless today, nor the runtime image needs a git binary of its own for materialization. The runtime's own git, which the agent uses, is whatever the runtime image ships.
- A session whose plan has a `gitClone` source is rejected at create when its pool has no git path (a pool with `egressProfile: restricted` without `gitAccess`). The check reads the final pool at the post-chain point of [RT-2](runtime-routing-design.md#rt-2-the-interceptor-contract), so a route or an interceptor that moves the session onto such a pool is refused there, with the last modifier named, as "Effective policy", **Where the pool checks run**, states.
- A default clone on a tenant-derived runtime ([DR-9](tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md#dr-9-default-git-clones), default git clones, in [`tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`](tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md)) is a `gitClone` source once composed, and these rules apply to it. That design also runs the ref resolver's host check on a default clone with a full commit. A default clone's `write` value adds to the session's writable set at bind, under the rules here. A default clone declares no store: a `gitRepository` asset of the runtime's `sharedAssets` fills one on the owned pool, under "Warm stores" in that design, and serves the default clone as it serves any `gitClone` source of its URL. An operator runtime may also carry default clones, with `write: none` (decision 67).
- The helper clones into a fresh directory under `/workspace/.lenny-build/`, with `git init --template=`, `-c core.hooksPath=/dev/null -c core.fsmonitor=false` on every command, and no command run in a target with a configuration the target supplied, and it runs all its git work there, including setting `origin`. It then moves the result to the target under §14's path collision rule and the ordering of [WM-7](workspace-materialization-design.md#wm-7-ordering-and-collisions), and replaces any `.git` at the target as a unit. Every later git step on the repository runs in the helper as the slot UID too: the index refresh, the capture copy before a checkpoint's first pass, and restore completion ([WM-10](workspace-materialization-design.md#wm-10-git)). A client's clone at `.` meets default files and default clones, and a `.git/config`, `commondir`, or hook already in the target could run code only at the slot UID, which every session file already reaches through the agent, so the rule protects the plan's outcome rather than a platform credential, and such a file still must not survive the move. The helper opens every write, move, and removal with `openat2` under `RESOLVE_BENEATH | RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS | RESOLVE_NO_XDEV`, because a restored symbolic link chain passes the lexical target checks (F-13.4.24). Before every checkout, the helper runs the tree-size check: it streams the tree's listing under its own git directory, charges each entry as 0111's rule 2 charges it with the total running across the plan's clones and files, and stops with `git_tree_limit` at the remaining workspace bytes, at `adapter.maxCloneTreeEntries`, or at the run's deadline, because a fetch cap bounds packed bytes alone, a tree can reuse subtrees, and a commit a store holds causes no fetch. The adapter reports `git_tree_limit` to the session as `UPLOAD_ARCHIVE_LIMIT_EXCEEDED`, a slot-reported reason. The adapter's per-slot disk watch, which fails the slot's session at `adapter.diskWatchThresholdPercent` of the volume's `sizeLimit`, 90% by default, is the backstop ([SI-10](session-isolation-design.md#si-10-what-stays-shared)). At the end of the run, once more than 1 s has passed since the last checkout, the helper refreshes the index, because without it git wrote 2,342 duplicate loose objects, about 28 MB, into the slot: git cannot freshen objects in a read-only store **[measured]** (spike 1 of [`session-isolation-design.md`, "Validation record"](session-isolation-design.md#validation-record)). Proposal 3 tests this, including alternates under the move, which 0111 writes as an absolute path under `/lenny/git-stores`. No test of the helper has run on this path.
- The tree checks that the archive gate applied are covered as described under "Residual risks and limits", **Tree validation**.

The 0111 open item on workspace ownership is settled (decision 51). Under [SI decision D9](session-isolation-design.md#decisions) every entry of a slot's workspace belongs to the slot UID: the helper checks a repository out as the slot UID, and a restore through the slot's broker creates every entry as the slot UID with its recorded mode ([IC-5](incremental-checkpoint-design.md#ic-5-restore-derive-download-and-seal)), after which `restore-complete` rewires the repository's alternates as the slot UID. The runtime's git therefore never meets a repository it does not own, and no repository needs to be group-writable.

#### Push limits and commit identity

- **`allowedRefPatterns`** on a VCS pool, and on a session's `writableRepositories` entry within it, limits which refs a session can push. Hosts cannot enforce this through a repository-scoped token, so `netagent` reads the ref update commands of `git-receive-pack`, which it already intercepts, and refuses the whole push if any ref falls outside the patterns. It also refuses ref deletion unless the pool allows it. Whether a push is forced cannot be determined from the commands alone, so that remains for the host's branch protection. The limit is enforced in the pod, and a tenant's host-side branch protection remains the outside control. Each push writes a `git.operation` record.
- **Push options.** `netagent` refuses a `git-receive-pack` request using the `push-options` capability and strips that capability from the ref advertisement, so git reports the failure before sending a pack. A VCS pool's `allowedPushOptions` list, empty by default, can admit an option. [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Git push options"](user-git-identity-design.md#git-push-options) owns the rule and the field, and this design carries the refusal in EG-3. GitLab's page lists `merge_request.auto_merge`, `ci.skip`, `secret_push_protection.skip_all`, and `security_policy.bypass_reason`. This needs bounded parsing of the section between the ref commands and the pack, which `netagent` reads on the terminated connection.
- **What the limit covers.** It covers git on a covered host, by any route to `netagent`: under the rendered config, with `http.proxy` overridden to the same listener, by `curl` over HTTPS through the proxy, and in an absolute-form plain HTTP request. With `http.proxy` unset there is no route, except on a pool with `egressProfile: internet`, whose raw sockets bypass `netagent`, and at the egress proxy with no credential the answer is 407 **[measured]**. On an uncovered host the limit does not hold, as **Uncovered git hosts** states. A write-scoped REST credential can also create refs through a host's API, which is a stated residual, and host-side branch protection is the outside control.
- **Commit identity.** A VCS pool can set the author and committer that the session's git configuration gives each repository under that pool, through one identity file per VCS pool ("Git configuration", decision 65), so agent commits are distinguishable and consistent.
- **GitLab bot accounts.** A GitLab project access token creates a bot user, so minting one per session fills the instance with bots. A pool can therefore reuse tokens, keyed on tenant, pool, project, and scope, so a read session never holds a write-capable token and no token crosses a tenant. A reused token rotates at a set lifetime, 7 days by default, and is revoked when the repository leaves the allowlist, `allowWrite` turns off, the pool is deleted, the tenant is erased, or an admin asks. A reused token outlives the session, so a compromised `netagent` could keep it until then, and it is an exception to P2. Reuse therefore needs the `storedTokenPush` grant, and per-session minting stays the default.

### Git configuration

Git keeps its remote URLs, so `git remote -v`, `gh`, and `glab` read the upstream host. The rendered git config names the local egress endpoint, the pod trust bundle, and a rule per covered host for SSH remotes:

```
[http]
    proxy = http://127.0.0.1:3128
    sslCAInfo = /run/netagent/ca-bundle.pem
    followRedirects = initial
[credential]
    helper =
[transfer]
    fsckObjects = true
[gc]
    auto = 0
[maintenance]
    auto = false
[url "https://github.com/"]
    insteadOf = git@github.com:
    insteadOf = ssh://git@github.com/
[include]
    path = /run/netagent/credentials/gitconfig
```

The file is pod-wide and holds only what every session on the pod shares, and `netagent` re-renders it when the tenant's covered hosts change. Its last section includes `/run/netagent/credentials/gitconfig`, an absolute path that each slot container resolves on its own `slot-<n>-netcred` mount, so every per-session value sits in the slot's own file and no slot reads another slot's. `netagent` writes that file when the session binds, rewrites it when the session's binding changes, and empties it at the slot's release. It selects the session's commit identity per repository (decision 65). `netagent` writes one identity file for each VCS pool that serves the session on the same volume, and the session's file includes each one under `includeIf "hasconfig:remote.*.url:<pattern>"`, with one condition for each of the pool's repository prefixes in its HTTPS form and in each SSH form that the host's `sshForms` entry maps, so a repository takes the identity of the pool whose prefix matches one of its remotes. In the identity file of a VCS pool with `prAuthoring: user`, `author.name` and `author.email` name the linked user's author identity, which the gateway resolves at bind and the pod binding carries, `committer.name` and `committer.email` name the pool's commit identity, and `commit.template` names a template on the same volume that adds the `Lenny-Session` trailer ([`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Commit authorship"](user-git-identity-design.md#commit-authorship)). In the identity file of any other VCS pool both name the pool's commit identity. A repository none of whose remotes matches a pool's prefix takes no identity from these files. The pinned git supports the condition, which git added in version 2.36. A runtime image whose own git is older than 2.36 ignores the condition, so its commits take no pool identity, and the runtime author guide states this. git skips an include whose file is absent, which is the case in `lenny-store-fill` and the adapter, because neither mounts a slot's credentials volume. git reads both files on each invocation.

- **Remotes.** `https://github.com/org/repo`, a remote on a repository, a clone the agent makes, and a remote added later all stay as written, with a CONNECT to the host. The same holds for submodules on covered and uncovered hosts **[measured]**.
- **Hosts with no credentials.** A public host such as kernel.org needs no credential binding. When it is in the effective policy and no pool or route covers it, git reaches it through an opaque tunnel. A git route to the mirror's public store or to `direct` makes it covered, and then a push is refused unless a VCS credential pool covers it.
- **SSH remotes.** Pods have no SSH egress. For each covered host, the config renders `insteadOf` rules from the `sshForms` entry in the host data, which maps a host's SSH remote forms to its HTTPS URL, so an SSH remote becomes the HTTPS URL wherever it appears: on a repository, in a clone the agent makes, and in a remote added later. With such a rule, git and `gh` both worked **[measured]**. Lenny code holds no per-provider logic. Round 3 checked the rule "keep the host and path, and switch the scheme" against public hosts:
  - It holds for github.com, gitlab.com (including subgroups), Codeberg, gitea.com, sourcehut, KDE, Debian Salsa, Launchpad, OpenDev Gerrit, and googlesource.com. The shipped host sets declare that identity mapping for those hosts.
  - The host changes for `ssh.github.com:443`, `altssh.gitlab.com:443`, GNOME's and freedesktop's `ssh.gitlab.*` hosts, Bitbucket Cloud (`ssh.bitbucket.org`), and kernel.org pushes (`gitolite.kernel.org`). An `sshForms` entry maps the SSH host to the HTTPS host.
  - The path changes for Azure DevOps (`ssh.dev.azure.com:v3/org/proj/repo` against `dev.azure.com/org/proj/_git/repo`), Bitbucket Data Center (`:7999/PROJ/repo` against `/scm/PROJ/repo`), Gerrit sites with a context path such as Wikimedia's `/r/`, and forges served under a relative URL. A rule rewrites a prefix alone, so an entry whose change sits past the prefix, as on Azure DevOps, renders a rule per declared repository **[design]**, and a deployer or tenant adds the entry with the git endpoint registration.
  - **The loss on an arbitrary host.** The rendered config cannot name a host it does not cover, so an SSH remote there gets an SSH failure with no `ERR` message and no `sshForms` mapping **[measured]**. The same holds on a covered host with no entry. `gh` still works on such a remote, because it reads the hostname.
  - The `origin` remote that materialization writes is always the HTTPS URL, so SSH forms appear only when an agent types one.
- **Remotes no rule matches.** SCP remotes with a user other than `git`, such as `alice@host:o/r`, and `git://` remotes match no rule **[measured]**. git then tries a direct connection, which the NetworkPolicy blocks, so those remotes fail closed.
- **`pushurl`.** With no rewrite, a `pushurl` is an ordinary URL. It goes through the proxy like any other, and a covered host applies the git path while an uncovered one is an opaque tunnel. The design uses no `pushInsteadOf`, because git-lfs and git disagree on applying it when a `pushurl` is set.
- **Overrides.** An agent owns its repository-local config and `git -c`. On a covered host none of these moves git off the git path **[measured]**: with `http.proxy` unset there is no route, except on a pool with `egressProfile: internet`, at the egress proxy with no credential the answer is 407, under `http.sslVerify=false` or `GIT_SSL_NO_VERIFY` enforcement holds, an `http.extraHeader` or URL token is replaced by the mint, a differing `Host` is refused, and a `.git/config` from a hook, `.gitmodules`, or `.lfsconfig` makes no token follow. Trust settings harm the agent alone, because an unverified upstream is still `netagent`.
- **`origin` remote.** Clones that the gateway produces have no `origin` remote today (`gitref.Clone`). The materialization step adds `origin` with the upstream URL, so push works without agent configuration.
- **Maintenance settings.** The configuration sets `gc.auto=0` and `maintenance.auto=false`, as [`incremental-checkpoint-design.md`](incremental-checkpoint-design.md) requests under its D6, because a repack rewrites packfiles and the next checkpoint then uploads the repository's history again (IC-1, "Git repositories"). This design owns the configuration and adopts the request (decision 59). The owner settled on 2026-11-13 that a runtime may override them (IC D6): the file is git's system scope through `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM`, so a runtime's global configuration, a repository's own configuration, or `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT` entries in the runtime's environment take precedence, and an agent can run `git gc` or `git maintenance` by hand. The cost falls on the session's own next checkpoint, which uploads the rewritten packs, and on no other session.
- **Who reads the file.** `lenny-materialize` names the file in `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM` for every git step it runs, and the runtime's own git reads it through the same variable, which the supervisor sets at launch. `lenny-store-fill` uses it as well.

### VCS pool fields

A VCS pool is a credential pool whose provider serves git hosts. This table lists every field it carries, with the section that defines each, including the fields [`user-git-identity-design.md`](user-git-identity-design.md) adds.

| Field | Meaning | Defined in |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `provider` | The provider, such as `github`, whose capability level decides what Lenny mints and allows | "Credentials", **Provider capability levels** |
| Provider credential | The Secret the Token Service mints from: `appId`, `privateKey`, and `installationId` for a GitHub App, or a stored token | "Credentials", **Minting** |
| Repository prefixes | The repository path prefixes the pool serves, which replace `hostPatterns` and the one-pool-per-host rule | "Credentials", **Repository routing** |
| Repository allowlist | The repositories sessions can use, each stored with its numeric identifier | "Credentials", **Write scope** |
| `allowWrite` | Whether the pool mints a write token for a repository a session declares writable | "Credentials", **Write scope** |
| `allowWorkflowEdits` | Whether a session can request `workflows: write`, on a provider that has that permission | "Credentials", **Write scope** |
| `allowStoredTokenPush` | Whether a `stored` provider's token can push, under the `storedTokenPush` grant | "Credentials", **Stored-token push** |
| `allowedRefPatterns` | The refs a session can push, and whether a ref can be deleted | "Push limits and commit identity" |
| `allowedPushOptions` | The git push options `netagent` admits, empty by default | [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Git push options"](user-git-identity-design.md#git-push-options), with the refusal under "Push limits and commit identity" |
| Commit identity | The author and committer the git configuration gives a session's repositories under the pool, through the pool's identity file | "Push limits and commit identity" |
| GitLab token reuse | Reuse of a project access token per tenant, pool, project, and scope, under the `storedTokenPush` grant | "Push limits and commit identity" |
| `userAuthorization`, `userAuthorization.linkProvider`, `serviceIdentities`, and `onAccessLost` | The per-user repository check, the service identities that skip it, and what a lost access does to a session | [`user-git-identity-design.md`](user-git-identity-design.md), [UG-2](user-git-identity-design.md#ug-2-repository-authorization) |
| `prAuthoring` and `pushIdentity` | Pull request authoring as the requesting user, and whose token carries a push | [`user-git-identity-design.md`](user-git-identity-design.md), [UG-3](user-git-identity-design.md#ug-3-pull-request-authoring) |
| `pinHosts` | Whether a VCS pool with `userAuthorization: required` sets credential pins on its hosts once EG-12 lands | [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Other tokens on these hosts"](user-git-identity-design.md#other-tokens-on-these-hosts) |
| `allowRuntimeHeldCredentials` | Whether a binding with `delivery: file` is admitted on the hosts the pool covers | [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Git and hosts"](user-git-identity-design.md#git-and-hosts) |
| `recheckIntervalSeconds` and `verdictMaxAgeSeconds` | A pool's narrowing of the repository check's Helm values | [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Configuration by scope"](user-git-identity-design.md#configuration-by-scope) |

## Credential delivery to runtimes

Pull requests, issues, releases, private package registries, and any other HTTP API a runtime calls go through the egress route with standard HTTP. Lenny adds no platform tool for them, and the delivery mechanism is generic: it knows hosts, headers, files, and request rules held as data, and it has no code for GitHub or any other product on this path. The one exception is git classification on every terminated host, so a marker match on a bound non-git API path, such as a path holding `.git/`, is refused.

### Credential bindings

A tenant admin configures access with a **credential binding**:

- `secret`: the source of the value, a typed union with a `kind`. It is a credential pool whose provider mints a token (for example a GitHub App, with a `permissions` ceiling such as `pull_requests: write`), a stored secret in the credential namespace, such as an API key, or `userCredential`, which names a tenant-defined type so that each session receives its own user's token (see [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md)), or `identityAssertion`, under which the Token Service mints for each session and host a short Lenny-signed token naming the session's tenant, user, and session, with `lifetimeSeconds` 300 by default and from 60 to 900 (see **Identity assertions.** under "Delivery modes"). The Token Service, `netagent`, and the decision service treat an unknown kind as absent, as they do `suspendedBy`.
- `hosts`: the exact hosts the value may be sent to. On a `userCredential` binding, these are a subset of the type's hosts, the hosts the user credential type declares or, for a linked type, its provider's API hosts, and host rules evaluate the type's hosts. Wildcards are refused, and each host must lie within the tenant's effective allow set, so a prompt-injected agent cannot make the tenant's key travel to a host the tenant never allowed. `netagent` never attaches the value to a request for any other host. A binding whose `secret` is a stored token, under either delivery, which includes a `userCredential` type with `source: registered`, whose tokens a tenant backend or the user writes to Lenny, is refused when any of its hosts is a git host that one of the tenant's VCS pools covers, because the long-lived token would otherwise reach that host's REST write routes with no path enforcement and outside the `storedTokenPush` grant; git on those hosts takes the git path with the pool's credential, and API access to them uses a minted token or a binding on the host's API hostname. The one exception is a `userCredential` binding under `delivery: file` when `allowRuntimeHeldCredentials` applies, which [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Other hosts"](user-git-identity-design.md#other-hosts) states. On the API host of a pool with `userAuthorization: required`, a stored secret is refused too, because it has no check, and setting `userAuthorization: required` on a pool suspends one that exists. A `userCredential` binding carries no CA and no `attach` of its own. Its `attach` comes from its type or link provider, and its CA from the host's endpoint or outbound host record, whose digest the token trust record holds.
- `attach`: how the value goes on the request, as a header template. Examples are `Authorization: Bearer {{secret}}`, `Authorization: Basic {{base64 user ":" secret}}`, and `X-Api-Key: {{secret}}`. A binding has no placeholder of its own: a tool sees the profile's constant `lenny-injected` in place of the secret (see **Placeholders under `delivery: inject`.** below). An `identityAssertion` binding's `attach` defaults to `Authorization: Bearer {{secret}}`.
- `scope`: `always`, which attaches the binding to every session of the tenant, `onRequest`, which attaches it only to a session whose plan names it, or `poolAttach`, which a VCS pool's `prAuthoring: user` sets and which [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "The attach rule"](user-git-identity-design.md#the-attach-rule) defines. A `userCredential` binding cannot be `always`.
- `delivery`: `inject`, `federation`, `metadata`, or `file`. A `userCredential` binding takes `inject` or `file`. An `identityAssertion` binding takes `inject` alone, because the assertion exists so that the agent never holds it, and a write naming another delivery for it is refused with `400 VALIDATION_ERROR`.
- `requestRules`: optional, under `delivery: inject` only, data that refuses requests before the value is attached (see **Request inspection stage.** below).
- `audience` and a provider template, for `federation` delivery.
- `projections`: for `file` delivery, the extra file formats to render (see **Which files `netagent` writes.** below).

A session names its `onRequest` bindings and, for a minted token, can narrow the permissions. A mint for a binding with any write permission names only repositories with a `granted` writable entry. The session does not enumerate APIs. One binding covers one secret and its hosts, however many endpoints those hosts expose. A session holds at most one binding with `delivery: inject` per host, except that an `identityAssertion` binding may share a host with one other `inject` binding when their `attach` headers differ, so a service that needs an API key and the caller's identity takes them in two headers (O5); two such bindings with the same header name are refused. A session also holds at most one `userCredential` binding per type host, under any delivery, and a binding with `delivery: file` beside a binding with `delivery: inject` on one host is refused too, each at bind and evaluated on type hosts for a `userCredential` binding, because `netagent` would otherwise overwrite one with the other and an App binding could answer in place of a user's.

### Delivery modes

| Mode | Where the secret is | Who can read it | Rotation | Availability |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `inject` (default) | `netagent` memory only. The pod sees the placeholder. | No process in the runtime or adapter container | Transparent to the tool | Always available |
| `federation` | A 15-minute Lenny-issued JWT in a file, which the provider's SDK exchanges at the provider | Any process at the agent UID | `netagent` rewrites the token, and the SDKs re-read it on each exchange | Tenant opt-in behind the `agentReadableDelivery` grant. It is an exception to P3, limited to a short-lived token bounded by the tenant's trust conditions. |
| `metadata` | `netagent` memory, served on loopback in a provider's metadata endpoint format | Any process at the agent UID that reads the endpoint's token file | Each fetch returns current credentials, and the SDKs refresh before expiry | Tenant opt-in behind the `agentReadableDelivery` grant. A provider plugin; AWS ships first. |
| `file` | A file in `/run/netagent/credentials`, written by `netagent` | Any process at the agent UID | `netagent` swaps the directory atomically, and the tested tools re-read their files on each invocation | Tenant opt-in behind the `agentReadableDelivery` grant, or `userCredentialDelivery` for a `userCredential` binding. It is an exception to P3. |
| Environment variable | Never used | n/a | n/a | Never offered |

**`inject`** keeps the secret out of the pod. The tool sends a request carrying the placeholder, or no credential, and `netagent` sets the header from the `attach` template when the request is for one of the binding's hosts. The check is on the request's authority, derived once: `:authority` on HTTP/2, and on HTTP/1.1 the request-target's host when the request is in absolute form, with a `Host` header that disagrees refused. The authority, with its port, must equal the bound host exactly, and the check is not on the CONNECT authority alone: on a shared edge, a request for `attacker.example` inside a connection to `api.example.com` would otherwise carry the injected header to the attacker's origin. `netagent` opens the upstream connection with the bound host as the server name and validates the upstream certificate against the public roots and the deployer CA only, never against the per-pod CA or a `tenantOverride.caBundle`, which sits in the per-pool TLS interception bundle and in no record. A binding or endpoint that carries its own CA adds that CA for its hosts only. `netagent` overwrites the header rather than substituting text inside it, so encoded forms such as Basic authentication work, and a placeholder copied into a request body or query is never replaced. A request to any other host leaves `netagent` unchanged, where the placeholder is meaningless. A stage between the authority check and the attach step inspects the request (see **Request inspection stage.** below). Two transports reach `netagent` in plain text:

- **Plain HTTP to a bound host.** A tool configured to call `http://<host>/...` sends an absolute-form request through `HTTP_PROXY`. `netagent` attaches the header and forwards the request over TLS through `lenny-egress`, so the cleartext stays on loopback. No CA is involved. Fly.io's tokenizer uses the same upgrade. `netagent` reads these requests' heads directly, because Go's `net/http` drops `Host` on an absolute-form request, and the check that `Host` and the target agree needs both. A smart HTTP or LFS path to a covered host takes the git path, and the rules for credential-pinned and read-only hosts apply, as they do inside a terminated tunnel. The third validation round showed that `glab` with `api_protocol: http` sends exactly this request through `HTTP_PROXY`, and that curl does so for `http://` URLs.
- **TLS termination for bound and covered hosts only.** For tools that hard-code HTTPS (for example `gh`, and git), `netagent` terminates TLS for the binding's hosts and the tenant's covered hosts with a per-pod CA and passes every other host through as an opaque tunnel. Each client CONNECT to a terminated host gets its own decision, `netagent` reuses a pooled upstream connection only for a CONNECT that decision allows, and a deny closes the pool for that host, because round 12 saw three new CONNECTs succeed with no decision after the policy stopped allowing the host. `netagent` generates the CA at first start and keeps its key in its private emptyDir, so a restart reuses it and the runtime's trust stays valid. The CA carries X.509 name constraints that permit only the hosts `netagent` terminates: those of the pod's bindings with `delivery: inject`, its covered hosts, and any credential-pinned or read-only hosts, and `netagent` re-issues it when a binding adds a host. A process that loaded the trust anchor before a host was known does not see the new constraints, and a client that enforces them refuses that host. The trust anchor therefore carries name constraints only when the runtime launches after bind and serves one binding set. Every runtime process does: it launches at bind, after materialization and setup, and serves one session ([SI-3](session-isolation-design.md#si-3-runtime-processes-slot-release-and-setup-commands)), on a pod with one slot or several and on a recycled pod alike, so `netagent` always issues the constrained anchor there. On a pod with several slots the constraints are the union of the slots' hosts, and a slot's process loads the anchor after its own bind, so its own hosts are in it. No pod carries an unconstrained anchor (decision 62): the service-mode pod and the `type: mcp` pod, whose runtime process started before any binding, left with [`agent-runtimes-only-design.md`](agent-runtimes-only-design.md) (ARR-8). Warm-up work that runs before any binding, the store fill and the other warm fills, uses the warm list's hosts, which the anchor issued at pod start covers. `netagent` records the coordination generation of the binding it has applied for each slot's session in that slot's binding list on `slot-<n>-netcred`, and sends it to the adapter in an `applied` message on `CH-FETCHGRANT`, because the adapter mounts no slot's credentials volume. The adapter waits for the session's coordination generation before materialization, setup, or launching the runtime, up to the fetch retry window, so a constrained trust anchor is current when a process loads it. If the wait ends first, the session fails at finalize with `SESSION_CREATION_FAILED` and the reason `network_not_ready`. OpenSSL, Go, rustls, BoringSSL, GnuTLS, NSS, .NET, and Chromium enforce constraints on a trust anchor, so for those clients a leaked key cannot sign for another DNS name. The CA also excludes every IP range, because a DNS-only constraint admits a leaf for an IP address in all of those except GnuTLS. A subdomain of a permitted host stays within the constraint in all of them, because X.509 has no exact-host form. Java's PKIX ignores a trust anchor's extensions, so JVM clients would trust a leaked key for any name; the limit is per pod and matters only after a `netagent` or node compromise, and the documentation states it. It writes the certificate into `/run/netagent` before the runtime starts (native sidecar ordering, validated in round 3), and the client proxy profile adds it to the CA trust variables. Vercel Sandbox, Cloudflare Sandbox, Daytona, E2B, and the Claude Code cloud GitHub proxy use the same pattern. Round 3 validated it with mitmproxy 12.3.1 standing in for `netagent` (see "Validation record"), and the spike ran git, `gh`, and `glab` through it on a stub.

**LLM provider hosts.** A binding with `delivery: inject` on an LLM provider's host is an ordinary binding and needs no grant beyond one, and a call that a runtime sends to a provider outside the gateway's LLM proxy is egress traffic like any other, under the same decisions, records, and ceilings (LU-4; OD-8 and OD-15 of [`llm-upstreams-and-cost-budgets-design.md`](llm-upstreams-and-cost-budgets-design.md#decisions)).

**Identity assertions.** An operator service that a runtime reaches through egress often needs to know who is calling, and a binding with `secret: {kind: identityAssertion}` tells it without trusting anything the agent sends. [`connector-removal-design.md`, "CR-2 Identity assertion"](connector-removal-design.md#cr-2-identity-assertion) defines the token, and this design carries it as an `inject` secret **[design]**:

- **Mint and cache.** On a request to a bound host, `netagent` looks up an assertion keyed by the slot's session, the binding and its definition version, the host and port, and the coordination generation. When none is cached, or the cached one has passed two thirds of its lifetime, `netagent` asks the Token Service on the pod credential API, presenting its `lenny-token-service` audience token as for any binding secret. The Token Service checks the pod's binding to the session, builds the claims from the session record and never from the pod, signs an ES256 JWT with `typ: lenny-identity+jwt` under the tenant's identity key, records the mint in `credential.mint` with its `jti`, and returns the token with its `expiresAt`. `netagent` removes any header the client sent under the `attach` header's name, attaches the assertion, and scrubs it from responses as it scrubs every `inject` secret. It flushes a slot's assertions at every binding change, at the session's end, and at the slot's release. An assertion is minted per session, binding, and host rather than per request, so the Token Service is not on the path of every request (O2).
- **Claims.** `iss` is the tenant's identity issuer, `sub` is `lenny:<tenant>:user:<user-id>` or `lenny:<tenant>:service:<principal-id>`, and `aud` is `https://<host>` of the request, with `:<port>` when the port is not 443, so one binding naming several hosts yields one audience per host. The other claims are `iat`, `nbf`, `exp`, `jti`, `tenant`, `environment`, `user`, `principal_type`, `session`, `root_session`, `depth`, and `binding`, with plain Lenny identifiers.
- **Availability.** The binding becomes available at the `running` commit, as a `userCredential` binding does. The provisional binding of a resume does not grant it, because materialization needs no operator service. A delegated child receives it under the binding's `scope`, as it receives any tenant binding, and the child's assertions name the child, the same root, and the child's own depth.
- **Pods.** A recycled pod and a pod that has served a session can carry the binding, because the assertion is minted per session and flushed at the slot's release. On a pool with several slots, `netagent` attaches a slot's assertion only to connections attributed to that slot, and a fallback pool refuses the binding (O7), as **Fallback pools** under "Slot attribution and fetch grants" states.
- **Outage.** When the Token Service is unavailable, `netagent` uses a cached assertion until it expires and then answers a request to the bound host with `503`, as this design fails closed for every binding secret.
- **Impersonation.** A delegation tree that holds an `identityAssertion` binding refuses impersonated input with `403 FORBIDDEN` and the reason `impersonation_identity_assertion`, as [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`, "UC-3"](user-credential-delivery-design.md#uc-3-the-usercredential-binding) **Impersonation** states (O6), because the assertion lets a service act as the user.
- **Verification.** An operator service verifies an assertion against the published keys alone, under the checks of the removal design's CR-2 **What a verifier checks.**, among them that a service that serves every tenant takes the tenant from the key that verified the token and never from an unverified claim. A reference verifier in Go ships with the SDKs.

**Request inspection stage.** Between the authority check and the attach step, on both transports, `netagent` runs a stage receiving the method, raw path, headers, and a bounded body reader, which can refuse the request or replace the forwarded path, headers, body, and query string. With no rules it admits everything unchanged and streams. A binding's `requestRules` reach `netagent` resolved on the pod credential API, as data, with no provider code in `netagent`: REST method and path patterns, GraphQL top-level mutation names, body text patterns, per-flavour paths, request forms, a default of allow or deny, and, under a default of deny, the `reads` setting that decides whether a read needs an allow rule. A binding whose rules have no `preset` key, on the API host of a VCS pool, resolves to the preset of the flavour that pool's provider and host derive, and only `preset: none` clears it. Classification runs first, under "Git on covered hosts", so a smart HTTP path on a terminated host never reaches this stage. Under rules, `netagent` fails closed on path spelling and forwards the canonical path. They apply under `delivery: inject` alone, and they cannot apply to a token the runtime holds. Unparseable GraphQL is refused, and the printed document is forwarded. Under rules, `netagent` strips credential headers and forwards the rest. Presets of rules ship as data, as the provider templates do. [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md) defines all of this, including who can clear a preset, except the flavour's default preset on a VCS pool's API host, which [`user-git-identity-design.md`, "Request rules on VCS hosts"](user-git-identity-design.md#request-rules-on-vcs-hosts) defines. [`github-credential-addon-design.md`, "Preset body"](github-credential-addon-design.md#preset-body) and [`gitlab-credential-addon-design.md`, "Preset body"](gitlab-credential-addon-design.md#preset-body) hold each preset's body and evidence. A rule refusal goes to `egress.local`, with the reason `not_listed` when no allow rule admits the request, as [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`, "Allow-list mode"](user-credential-delivery-design.md#allow-list-mode) states.

`inject` cannot serve these cases, which need `federation`, `metadata`, or `file` delivery:

- Request signing computed from the secret on the client, such as cloud request signing (`federation` or `metadata`) or HMAC-signed requests (`file`).
- mTLS client certificates.
- Tools that pin certificates or use compiled-in roots, under TLS termination.
- Tools that validate the token format locally and reject the placeholder.
- OAuth flows in which the client must hold a refresh token.

A response that reflects the secret (an echo endpoint, or an error that quotes the request) would carry it into the pod. `netagent` scrubs the value and each rendered form of it from response headers, bodies, and WebSocket server frames on bound hosts, because a reflected Basic header carries the secret only in encoded form, which round 10 showed the exact value misses:

- It rewrites `Accept-Encoding` to the encodings it can decode and refuses a response in any other, because a reflection compressed with `zstd` otherwise passes through unscrubbed. It decodes `gzip` and `deflate` bodies before scrubbing. Round 3 confirmed that reflections in plain, compressed, and finely chunked bodies never reached the client.
- A streaming response (SSE, or any body `netagent` does not buffer) is scrubbed with a window that holds only a suffix that is a proper prefix of the secret, so a secret split across chunks is still caught and an event that cannot begin a secret passes at once. Round 10 tested this against the fixed window first proposed here, which held back one byte less than the secret's length and so withheld each event's end until the next bytes: 503 ms of lag at 500 ms spacing and 10.2 s across a pause, against 1.1 ms and 2.7 ms for the prefix window, with no leak in the reflection runs, including 5-byte and 7-byte chunks and flushed gzip. Buffering the whole body instead delayed every SSE event to the end of the stream.
- WebSocket frames from the server need their own scrub. Without it, a reflected secret in a frame reached the client.
- A reflection re-encoded by the server, such as base64 or hex, defeats the scrub. The documentation states this as a residual risk.

`netagent` also strips hop-by-hop headers such as `Proxy-Connection` before forwarding, which mitmproxy passed upstream in the plain-HTTP test.

**Gates.** The opt-ins that place a credential in the agent's reach fall into risk classes, and each class has its own gate: `storedTokenPush` (a long-lived secret held by `netagent`), `agentReadableDelivery` (`file`, `federation`, and `metadata` of a tenant's credential), `userCredentialDelivery` (`file` of a person's token, under a `userCredential` binding, as [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md) defines), and `verbatimClientFiles` (tenant client config files that `netagent` writes into the pod verbatim, an arbitrary-content channel). [`tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`](tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md) adds `tenantDerivedRuntimes`, a gate of this form with parameters. A gate is a platform-admin grant per tenant rather than a deployment-wide switch, and the tenant admin then opts in per binding or pool, so enabling `file` for one tenant enables nothing for another and nothing in another class.

**`federation`** gives a runtime a short-lived identity token that it exchanges itself, at the provider, for the provider's own credentials. It serves SDKs that sign requests and so cannot use `inject`, and it is provider-neutral: Lenny's part is one JWT issuer and a set of file templates, and every provider that accepts OIDC workload identity federation consumes it through its own SDK. The clouds (AWS, GCP, and Azure), HashiCorp Vault, and most secrets managers accept it, and Kubernetes itself uses the same mechanism for its own workload identity.

- **Issuer.** The Token Service is an OIDC issuer with one issuer URL per tenant, `https://<lenny>/oidc/<tenant-id>`, keyed by the tenant identifier rather than the name. The identifier is caller-supplied at creation (`admin/tenants.go`), and the §12.8 tenant tombstone prevents its reuse, so a deleted and recreated tenant cannot inherit the trust the old one's admin configured; the issuer's uniqueness rests on that tombstone. Each tenant has its own federation key set, held in the Token Service's key store (`kms.go`) beside its other keys, signing nothing else, and destroyed when the tenant is deleted, so a federation token is useless at any Lenny endpoint and dies with its tenant. Rotation keeps the previous key published for the token lifetime plus the providers' JWKS caching period. The `/oidc/` path on the gateway ingress is reserved for the Token Service, and discovery must be reachable by the providers' token endpoints, which means a publicly routable ingress with a publicly trusted certificate; a deployment without one cannot offer `federation`, and uses `metadata` instead. A tenant's trust configuration names its own issuer, so another tenant's tokens fail the issuer check by construction, and the tenant admin needs no claim condition to keep P4. A binding deleted and recreated under the same name keeps its subject, so the tenant admin treats a binding name as the identity its provider role trusts.
- **Identity issuer and key sets.** Beside the federation issuer, the Token Service is a second issuer per tenant, `https://<lenny>/identity/<tenant-id>`, for identity assertions ([`connector-removal-design.md`, "CR-2 Identity assertion"](connector-removal-design.md#cr-2-identity-assertion), O4 and D8). Its ES256 key set is separate from the federation key set, signs nothing else, and is destroyed with the tenant. The Token Service serves, unauthenticated, `GET https://<lenny>/identity/<tenant-id>/.well-known/openid-configuration` with `issuer` and `jwks_uri`, `GET https://<lenny>/identity/<tenant-id>/jwks.json`, the tenant key set, and `GET https://<lenny>/identity/.well-known/jwks.json`, the deployment key set, which lists every key of every live tenant's key set at the same moments the tenant key sets list them, each with `kid`, `kty`, `crv`, `x`, `y`, `alg: ES256`, `use: sig`, and a `tenant` member naming its tenant, while the Helm value `tokenService.identityAssertion.deploymentKeySet.enabled` is `true`, its default. With `false`, the deployment key set route answers `404`, rotation and tenant deletion act on the tenant key sets alone, and a service that serves every tenant verifies through the issuer-prefix method of that design's CR-2, fetching each tenant's key set by the token's issuer (D9 of that design); a service still configured with the deployment key set URL refuses every assertion, so it fails closed. Every key set response carries `Cache-Control: max-age=300`. A `kid` begins with the tenant identifier, so it is unique across the deployment, and a tenant identifier cannot begin with a dot, so `.well-known` names no tenant. The Token Service rotates a tenant's identity key every `tokenService.identityAssertion.keyRotationDays`, 30 by default. A new key is published in both sets at least the cache period plus 60 seconds before its first use, and the previous key stays in both for 900 seconds, the longest assertion lifetime, plus the cache period after its last use. An emergency rotation through the admin API drops the previous key from both at once. Tenant deletion removes the tenant's keys from the deployment key set, and its issuer then answers `404`. The `/identity/` path on the gateway ingress is reserved for the Token Service, as `/oidc/` is. It must be reachable from wherever the operator's services run, which for an internal service can be a cluster-internal address, and an operator who must not disclose tenant identifiers turns the deployment key set off or serves the path only there, because the deployment key set lists every live tenant's identifier while it is enabled.
- **Token.** For a session with a binding with `delivery: federation`, `netagent` requests a JWT from the Token Service on the pod credential API and writes it to `/run/netagent/credentials/<name>/token`. The claims are the tenant's issuer, the binding's `audience`, the stable subject `lenny:<tenant>:<binding>`, and separate `pool` and `session` claims for audit. The subject is stable because AWS trust policies condition only on `aud`, `sub`, and `amr`, and Azure requires an exact subject match. The token lasts 15 minutes, and `netagent` rewrites it at two thirds of its lifetime. The SDKs re-read the file on each exchange; the Azure SDKs cache it for 5 to 10 minutes, which is within that lifetime.
- **Trust.** The tenant admin configures the provider to trust the tenant's issuer for that audience and subject, and grants the resulting identity the provider role or permissions the agent needs. This is the same step a platform team performs for a Kubernetes cluster's workload identity today. Where the provider supports it (AWS `aws:SourceIp`, GCP and Azure conditional access), the tenant also binds the exchange to the egress address of its egress group, which makes a token taken out of the pod useless elsewhere.
- **Client configuration.** The binding's provider template renders the provider's standard client configuration file into the binding's directory on the slot's own credentials volume, naming the token path, and `netagent` adds the variable that names that file to the session launch variables in the slot's binding list ("Injected configuration", layer 2), which the supervisor sets when it launches the session's runtime process. Lenny ships templates for the three clouds as data, and a deployer adds others.

| Provider | File rendered | Launch variable | Per-tenant values | Validation (round 4) |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| AWS | A shared config profile with `role_arn`, `web_identity_token_file`, `role_session_name`, `region`, and optional `services` endpoints | `AWS_CONFIG_FILE` | All in the file | boto3, AWS SDK for Go v2, JavaScript v3, and Java v2 assumed the role, signed requests, and re-read a changed token on refresh |
| GCP | An `external_account` JSON with `audience`, `token_url`, `credential_source.file`, and optional impersonation | `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS` | All in the file, except the project lookup below | google-auth for Python, Node, and Go exchanged the token and re-read it on refresh |
| Azure | None. The SDKs read `AZURE_TENANT_ID`, `AZURE_CLIENT_ID`, and `AZURE_FEDERATED_TOKEN_FILE` from the environment only | The three variables | The tenant ID and client ID cannot come from a file | azure-identity for Python, Node, and Go exchanged the token and re-read it after their cache expired |

Two caveats come from the validation:

- The Python and Node Google libraries look up the project at `cloudresourcemanager.googleapis.com` during initialization. The `googleapis` host set includes that host, so the lookup succeeds through the proxy when the policy allows it. Otherwise the binding sets `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT`, which `netagent` writes as a session launch variable.
- Azure's identifiers cannot come from a file, so `netagent` writes `AZURE_TENANT_ID`, `AZURE_CLIENT_ID`, and `AZURE_FEDERATED_TOKEN_FILE` as session launch variables in the slot's binding list, and the supervisor sets them when it launches the session's runtime process, on a shared pool as on a tenant-owned pool. Runtime code can instead pass the identifiers from `binding.json` to the credential constructor through the SDK accessor, and the validation confirmed that all three Azure SDKs accept them as constructor arguments.

The JWT is readable by any process at the agent UID, which makes `federation` an exception to P3 in the same way as `file`, limited to a 15-minute token whose use the tenant's trust conditions bound. The `agentReadableDelivery` grant controls it. The provider exchange is an HTTPS request to the provider's token endpoint, which the effective policy must allow. A token taken out of the pod can be exchanged from anywhere within its lifetime unless the trust binds the source address, so the documentation recommends that binding and roles with the narrowest permissions.

**`metadata`** emulates a provider's instance metadata or container credentials endpoint on loopback, for a tenant that cannot configure federation trust. The Token Service holds the provider credential and mints the short-lived credentials that `netagent` serves. Round 3 and round 4 validated the three formats: the AWS container credentials endpoint with boto3, the AWS CLI, and the Go, JavaScript, and Java SDKs; the GCE metadata server with google-auth for Python, Node, and Go; and the Azure App Service managed identity endpoint with azure-identity for Python, Node, and Go. Each format needs its own paths, headers, and response document, so `metadata` is a provider plugin in `netagent` with a stated interface rather than data. Lenny ships the AWS plugin, because AWS is the one provider whose endpoint the SDKs restrict to loopback, and the other two follow when a deployer needs them. Three details from the validation hold for every plugin: a token file must not end in a newline, the Python Google library needs `GCE_METADATA_IP` as well as `GCE_METADATA_HOST`, and the emulator must serve each probe path the SDK sends before the token path.

**`file`** writes the secret into the pod.

No standard governs how a platform hands a secret to a workload as a file. The practices in common use are these:

| Practice | Examples | Fit for Lenny |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| One file per secret in a directory that an environment variable names | Kubernetes Secret volumes, Docker and Compose secrets in `/run/secrets/<name>`, and systemd credentials in `$CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY` | Generic. Any program can read a file, and Kubernetes rotates such directories by an atomic symlink swap. |
| `.netrc` | curl, git, GNU wget, Python requests, pip, and the Go toolchain | Host-scoped Basic credentials only. Round 3 found uneven support: curl needs `--netrc`, httpx needs explicit `NetRCAuth`, and BusyBox wget, npm, Node fetch, and Java ignore it. Only requests, pip, and Go read the `NETRC` variable. |
| OIDC workload identity federation | Kubernetes service account tokens consumed by AWS IAM Roles for Service Accounts, GKE Workload Identity, Azure Workload Identity, and Vault JWT auth | A standard, provider-neutral mechanism with one issuer on the platform side. This is the `federation` mode. |
| Cloud workload credential endpoints | AWS container credentials, the GCP metadata server, and the Azure managed identity endpoint | Standard within each cloud's SDKs, and short-lived, but each has its own format. This is the `metadata` mode. |
| Tool-native config files | `gh` `hosts.yml`, `.npmrc`, `pip.conf`, and Docker `config.json` | Each tool owns its format. |
| Exec credential helpers | git credential helpers, Docker credential helpers, kubectl exec plugins, and AWS `credential_process` | Each is a tool-specific protocol, and each needs a helper binary in the image. |

The `file` contract follows the first practice:

- The launch environment sets `LENNY_CREDENTIALS_DIR=/run/netagent/credentials`. The path sits outside `/run/lenny`, which §13.1 reserves for the credential tmpfs.
- Each binding with `delivery: file` of the session gets a directory, `<name>/`. It holds `value`, the raw secret with no trailing newline, and `binding.json`, with the name, hosts, `attach` template, `expiresAt`, and a version counter.
- `netagent` replaces a binding's directory atomically by writing a new directory and swapping a symlink, as the kubelet does for Secret volumes. A reader that opens the file on each use always sees a complete value.
- The files have mode 0444 in an emptyDir that only `netagent` writes. The controller renders one credentials volume per slot, `slot-<n>-netcred`, whose name [`agent-pod-layout-design.md`, "PL-5"](agent-pod-layout-design.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction) owns, a memory-backed emptyDir whose `sizeLimit` of 8 MiB PL-5 sets, mounted read-write into `netagent` at `/run/netagent-slots/<n>` and read-only at `/run/netagent/credentials` into slot `n`'s container alone, so a session's `delivery: file` values reach no other slot ([SI-7](session-isolation-design.md#si-7-credentials-manifests-and-other-shared-files)). `netagent` writes a session's files into the volume of the slot the binding names, and empties it at the slot's release before the next session binds.

**Which files `netagent` writes.** `netagent` writes the generic directory for each binding with `delivery: file` and nothing else, unless the binding lists `projections`.

- Lenny ships one projection, `netrc`. It is a single file at `/run/netagent/credentials/netrc`, on the slot's own credentials volume, with an entry for each binding whose `attach` template is Basic. The profile sets `NETRC` to that path on every pod, and the file is empty when the session has no such binding. It serves requests, pip, and Go.
- Lenny ships no tool-specific projections. A deployer adds one as a file template in the client proxy profile, such as a `gh` `hosts.yml`.
- `glab` refuses a config file unless its mode is exactly 0600, which a file written by the `netagent` UID cannot give the agent UID. `glab` therefore uses `inject` with TLS termination, configured through `GITLAB_HOST` and a `GITLAB_TOKEN` placeholder.

**Translation by the runtime.** A runtime that builds its own clients reads the generic directory, through the SDK accessor (see "Runtime SDKs") or directly, and configures those clients itself. Tool knowledge then stays with the runtime author, who knows which tools the image carries. Lenny adds no template engine or tool formats to the SDKs, for two reasons:

- A command the agent runs in a shell never passes through runtime code, so a translation in the runtime would not reach it.
- A rendering step in the runtime would have to watch for rotation.

**Placeholders under `delivery: inject`.** `inject` needs no file. The tested tools send no credential, or a placeholder, and `netagent` overwrites the header. A tool that refuses to run without a token (round 3: `gh`) gets a placeholder through an environment variable in the profile, such as `GH_TOKEN` or `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN`. `netagent` ignores the placeholder's value, so the placeholder is the constant `lenny-injected` rather than a per-binding value, and it can be fixed at pod creation.

**Environment variables** are never used for secrets, and §5.1 already forbids them. Compared with a file, an environment variable has these additional exposures:

- `/proc/<pid>/environ` keeps the start-time value even after the process unsets it.
- Every child process inherits it.
- Diagnostic reports include it, such as Node's report on an uncaught exception.
- The Lenny supervisor renders the runtime's environment as command-line arguments.
- It cannot be rotated without restarting the process.

OWASP's secrets management guidance and CWE-526 make the same recommendation. A placeholder in an environment variable is not a secret, so `inject` can expose one as `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN=lenny-injected`. Round 3 confirmed that `gh` refuses to run with no token and runs with an environment token.

### Exfiltration controls

These controls land in EG-12, after the other proposals, and bytes out lands with the `egress.tunnel` stream. Hostname filtering leaves opaque tunnels, so an allowed host can carry data out (see "Residual risks and limits"). A published attack sent files to a trusted LLM API using an attacker's key. These controls narrow that channel for hosts where the platform or tenant turns them on:

- **Credential-pinned hosts.** A host set or binding can set `credentialPinned`, and a VCS pool with `userAuthorization: required` under [`user-git-identity-design.md`](user-git-identity-design.md) is a source of credential pins for its host and API hosts, with the per-session exception that document states. `netagent` then terminates TLS for those hosts, as it does for a bound host, and refuses a request when the host has no binding. An attacker's key cannot be used there, under these rules:
  - The match uses the shared canonicalizer (case and default port), so `API.EXAMPLE.COM:443` cannot miss the credential pin and get an opaque tunnel. Inside a credential-pinned connection, a request whose authority differs is refused, where `inject` alone would pass it through without attaching a secret.
  - The binding's template overwrites its header, and a per-host credential list strips other credential headers and query parameters, such as `?private_token=`. The list is data in the host set, and a host with no list strips every header that the shipped list names.
  - WebSocket and other upgrades are refused, because their frames escape these checks.
  - On a credential-pinned git host with no binding, a smart HTTP path takes the git path first, and other requests are refused. A read-only host admits `info/refs` and `git-upload-pack` as reads and refuses `git-receive-pack`, because classification runs before the method rule.
  - A credential pin names host names, and a provider's alias hosts are credential-pinned with it from host data. Another name for the same service, or an agent's own name pointing at the host's address, is outside it, as **Uncovered git hosts** states.
  - The credential-pinned hosts come from the Token Service on the pod credential API, as a binding's `hosts` do, so a compromised decision service cannot remove a credential pin. The decision service reads the same definitions and refuses a pod's CONNECT to a credential-pinned, read-only, or covered host unless `netagent` marks it as coming from a terminated connection, with a header that `netagent` sets and strips from agent input. Service identities (the gateway's ref resolver, the mirror, and the Token Service) have no `netagent` and carry no agent-supplied credential, so the check does not apply to them. A level 2 proxy must pass this header to its egress decision API call and drop it toward any upstream, as `lenny-egress` does. A level 2 proxy must also pass the asserted route, under "Credentials", **Deployments without a central proxy**. A `netagent` that has not read its list yet, for any reason, therefore gets a 403 for those hosts instead of an opaque tunnel, and no other host is affected. The marker for covered hosts lands in EG-3, and this control adds credential-pinned and read-only hosts to it. A compromised `netagent` can forge the marker, and a compromised decision service can allow the tunnel, which are the stated limits of each. A trailing dot canonicalizes away.
  - These limits remain, and the documentation states them: a credential carried in a request body, and an upload the binding's own key authorizes into a place an attacker can read.
- **Read-only hosts.** Under the same termination and the same list source, a host can allow only `GET` and `HEAD`, which closes publish and upload paths on a registry the pool only needs to read. A tool that needs `POST` for reads must be listed separately.
- **Bytes out.** Each `egress.tunnel` record carries bytes in each direction, and an alert fires on a per-session threshold. This detects and does not prevent, and it cannot see inside a tunnel. `netagent` local tunnel accounting serves the same purpose at level 1. Traffic to an operator service, such as an MCP server that a runtime's harness calls, passes through `netagent` and `lenny-egress` like any other egress, so the alert counts it, as "Residual risks and limits", **Operator services**, states.

These controls act in `netagent`, so they apply to traffic that passes through it. A pool with `egressProfile: internet` permits raw sockets that bypass it, so the validators refuse a host set's or binding's credential pin on pools with `egressProfile: internet`, and that pool's sessions stay outside the credential pin from a VCS pool with `userAuthorization: required`, which posture reports.


## Interfaces

| Interface                 | Between                                                                    | Protocol                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Defined by                                |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- |
| I1. Local egress endpoint | tools in the pod, including git, and `netagent` | HTTP CONNECT and absolute-form plain HTTP on loopback, no authentication | Standard |
| I2. Proxy hop             | `netagent` and the central proxy (Lenny default or a deployer replacement) | HTTP CONNECT over TLS with `Proxy-Authorization: Basic`. The password is the service account token for the default or a level 2 proxy, and the identity's current credential for a level 1 proxy.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Standard                                  |
| I3. Egress decision API   | proxy, git endpoint, or Token Service and `lenny-egress-authz`             | Envoy ext_authz v3, in both its gRPC and HTTP forms. A documented mapping to the OpenID AuthZEN Authorization API 1.0 (final since 2026-01-12) is optional, because no proxy calls AuthZEN today.                                                                                                                                                                                          | Standard (ext_authz)                      |
| I4. Egress policy document | Lenny and a level 1 proxy                                  | A versioned Lenny JSON document listing each credential identity with its allowed host patterns, ports, and credential verifiers, served by the gateway admin API. Lenny also publishes a plain-text domain list per identity in the External Dynamic List format that security gateways and firewalls ingest over HTTPS. No standard exists for per-identity egress allowlists. NPEP-133 `domainNames` selects pods rather than proxy identities. | Lenny, with EDL output                    |
| I5. Git endpoint          | `netagent` and a git service                                               | git smart HTTP over TLS, protocol v2 for fetch, with Basic or Bearer authentication                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | Standard                                  |
| I6. Upstream chaining | `lenny-egress` and a deployer upstream proxy | Forwarded HTTP CONNECT carrying `Proxy-Authorization: Basic` when the upstream accepts it. Identity is otherwise the egress group's source address. `X-Authenticated-User` and `X-Forwarded-For` are optional, for upstreams that read them on CONNECT or that decrypt. | Standard (de facto for security gateways) |
| I7. Pod binding API       | `netagent` and `lenny-egress-authz`                                        | A Lenny gRPC API over TLS, authenticated by the `lenny-egress` audience token. It covers the binding subscription (including the proxy Service to dial and the covered host set), the base-destination list, `netagent`'s local decision reports, and at level 1 a decision call for each CONNECT.                                                                                                                                                                  | Lenny                                     |
| I8. Pod credential API    | `netagent` and the Token Service; also the mirror on its mTLS identity     | A Lenny gRPC API over TLS, authenticated by the `lenny-token-service` audience token (or the mirror's client certificate). It covers requests for git tokens, binding secrets, federation JWTs, `metadata` credentials, and level 1 proxy credentials, and its responses carry the binding's authoritative hosts and attach template, `cacheUntil`, `handling`, any resolved `requestRules`, and for a `userCredential` binding the expected route target for its list and the CA from the token trust record the Token Service compared. The Token Service consults `lenny-egress-authz` on I3 for the scope decision. | Lenny                                     |

## Configuration by scope

### Scopes and who manages them

| Central proxy implementation: the Envoy default or a replacement address and contract level                                                          | Chooses whether pod egress goes to the shipped Envoy `lenny-egress` to a deployer proxy that calls the decision service (level 2), or to a deployer proxy that enforces a published host list (level 1, not recommended; see "Level 1 replacement").                                                       | Deployment                                       | Platform admin (Helm)                                                   |                                                                                                                                                                                                      |

The decision pins their addresses, so the address filter keeps no exemption.                                                              |

A granted tenant's pods dial the group's proxy instead of the default (see "Defaults, replacement, and augmentation"). |

Today a tenant admin can change it on a pool shared with other tenants (§10.2 "Manage egress profiles"), and the design restricts that write. |

A tenant overlay can narrow it, and can extend it only when the platform admin allows that for the pool.                                            |
