Before a session binds, a pod's readable set is its warm list, for its pool and revision, plus the repositories this policy names. It applies before any tenant is known, so tenants cannot override it. Bound pods use the tenant warm-up additions instead.              |

Not available on concurrent-session pools.                                                                      |

Not available on concurrent-session pools.                                                                                                        |

Other designs own some settings that pass through this design's write path. `userCredentialDelivery` is one of the gates above, granted per tenant. `userCredentialTypes`, link providers, and `requestRules` on a binding are tenant settings under [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md), and `userAuthorization`, `prAuthoring`, `pinHosts`, `allowedPushOptions`, and `allowRuntimeHeldCredentials`, which builds on [UC-5](user-credential-delivery-design.md#uc-5-delivery-file)'s `delivery: file` for a user's token, on a VCS pool are tenant settings under [`user-git-identity-design.md`](user-git-identity-design.md). Presets are deployment data. Each goes through EG-9's write path.

Tenant admins hold most of the configuration surface. The platform admin sets the outer bounds (the ceiling, the SSRF ranges, private-address services, and the opt-in gates) and the settings that must be fixed at pod creation. Tenant admins can register services on public addresses themselves. The rule first followed the precedent of §9.3's MCP connector definitions, which [`connector-removal-design.md`, "CR-1 Removal"](connector-removal-design.md#cr-1-removal) removes, leaving §9.3 as a successor pointer, and it stands without that precedent. Services on private addresses need a platform registration, because they need a NetworkPolicy `ipBlock` on `lenny-egress` and a pinned address in the decision.

A tenant admin holding a platform grant creates derived runtimes and their owned pools, under [`tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`](tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md). Every other runtime and pool stays platform-global, and that design's [DR-1](tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md#dr-1-to-dr-3-prerequisites) removes `tenant-admin` writes on them, changing what §15.1 states today. A template pool supplies the ceiling, the warm-up policy, `tenantEgressProfiles`, and each other setting that needs a platform decision, and the values are copied to the owned pool, so a tenant cannot create a ceiling. `ownerTenantId` comes from draft 0111, which is on the branch `draft/0111-warm-workspace` and has not landed, so EG-9 carries it.

### Tenant pool overlays

Today a pool is one platform-global record. A tenant admin whose tenant has a grant edits that record in place (§10.2, the "own tenant" scoping note), so the edit changes the pool for every tenant that shares it. No per-tenant layer exists: `pool_tenant_access` holds only the grant. Per-tenant override layers exist for other resources, such as the tenant-level `leaseExtension` override (§8).

The design adds a **tenant pool overlay**: one record per pool and tenant, written by the tenant admin, which applies only to that tenant's pods. The platform admin controls each overridable setting with a per-pool flag, `tenantOverride`, whose values are `none`, `narrow` (the default), and `narrowOrExtend`.

- Narrowing reduces only the tenant's own access, so `narrow` is the default.
- Extending needs `narrowOrExtend`, set by the platform admin per pool and setting.
- A setting applied at pod creation (the NetworkPolicy label, environment variables, and the CA trust bundle) cannot vary per tenant on a shared pool, because warm pods exist before any tenant. The overlay can set those only on a pool the tenant owns (proposal 0111 `ownerTenantId`), where every pod belongs to the tenant from creation.
- The tenant admin's global write on a shared pool is restricted for the egress and git settings below. The tenant admin writes the overlay instead.

| Pool setting | Overlay on a shared pool | Overlay on a tenant-owned pool | Flag |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Egress ceiling | Narrow, or extend when allowed. The extension still excludes the SSRF ranges. | Same | `tenantOverride.egressCeiling` |
| Warm-up policy | None. Unbound pods have no tenant. Bound pods use the tenant warm-up additions. | Replace, within the ceiling | `tenantOverride.warmUp` |
| `gitAccess` | Disable, which the decision service enforces. Enabling needs the NetworkPolicy, which is fixed at creation. | Enable or disable | `tenantOverride.gitAccess` |
| `egressProfile` | None | Choose among the profiles the platform admin lists in `tenantEgressProfiles` | `tenantEgressProfiles` |
| Client proxy profile | File templates only, under the `verbatimClientFiles` grant, because a tenant template is an arbitrary-content channel | On a pool with `egressProfile: internet` that runs no `netagent`, environment variables named on an allowlist the chart ships, under "Tenant proxy variables", and on any pool, file templates, with the same grant for templates. A denylist stays open, because `LD_PRELOAD`, `NODE_OPTIONS`, `GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `PATH`, and `LD_AUDIT` all run code. | `tenantOverride.clientProfile` |
| CA trust bundle | None | Add the tenant's CA, such as its gateway's interception CA | `tenantOverride.caBundle` |
| Fetch size cap | Narrow, or extend when allowed | Same | `tenantOverride.fetchSizeCap` |
| Fetch retry window | Narrow | Same, within the pool's validated bound | `tenantOverride.fetchRetryWindow` |

**Tenant proxy variables.** The allowlist holds `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY`, and `NO_PROXY` in both letter cases, `npm_config_proxy`, `npm_config_https_proxy`, `npm_config_noproxy`, `YARN_HTTP_PROXY`, `YARN_HTTPS_PROXY`, and `CARGO_HTTP_PROXY`. The rules are:

- Each proxy value is an `http://` or `https://` URL with a host and an optional port, and `ALL_PROXY` is `http://` only. A URL carrying a user or a password is refused, because launch variables are rendered as arguments.
- A tenant's `NO_PROXY` and `npm_config_noproxy` add to the lists the controller derives and cannot replace them, so a base destination is never sent to the tenant's proxy.
- `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS` is refused, because `-javaagent` and `-XX:OnOutOfMemoryError` run code. The controller renders the JVM proxy properties from the tenant's `HTTPS_PROXY`.
- The trust variables are refused. `caBundle` adds the tenant's CA to the pod trust bundle those variables already name.
- A pool with `egressProfile: proxied` or `restricted` refuses the variables, as a Runtime `env` entry is refused on a pool with `egressProfile: proxied`, because the pod's other sockets are dropped. So does a pool with `egressProfile: internet` that runs `netagent`, because tools sent to the tenant's proxy would carry `inject` placeholders that only `netagent` swaps, and would skip the decision and its record. A tenant on those pools chains through an egress route.
- The test is on the pool's effective profile and `netagent` setting at pod creation. When an `egressProfile` overlay or a template change moves the pool out of the rule, the stored variables stop applying on pods created afterwards, and the API reports them as inactive.

On a pool created under a derivation grant, the rows only a tenant-owned pool has read as `none` unless the derivation grant entry's `ownedOverlays` list, which names the overlay rows an owned pool may use, lists them, because a copied flag was set with the shared pool in mind.

The overlay mechanism is general, and other pool settings, such as scaling, can adopt it later. This design defines it only for the egress and git settings above. Two edges are stated: a platform admin who lowers a flag from `narrowOrExtend` to `narrow` invalidates stored overlays that extend, and the write is refused until those overlays are reduced, except on a pool a derivation grant entry names and on its owned pools, where the write succeeds, the extending part stops applying, and the audit event `runtime.base_changed`, which tells each affected tenant, goes out, because one tenant's overlay must not block a platform write; and enabling `gitAccess` through an overlay on a tenant-owned pool changes the pods' NetworkPolicy label, which takes effect on the next pod roll, so the API reports it as pending until then.

### An open ceiling

A pool ceiling of `*` (any public host) with tenant policies as the effective control is a supported setup. Its safety rests on these points:

- The SSRF exclusions still apply, so `*` covers public addresses only, apart from the hosts a tenant routes to its own outbound connector. `*` matches those names too, and the SSRF reasoning does not apply to them, because they are resolved on the customer's side and never on the platform side. The tenant's policy must still allow such a host, and on a pool with a named ceiling only the `tenantOverride.outboundHosts` flag of [OC-2](tenant-outbound-connector-design.md#proposals) lets an overlay add one.
- A tenant's policy defaults to the platform's shipped default policy, which allows nothing beyond the default host sets.
- Every connection is decided and logged per tenant, so an open ceiling still produces a complete audit trail.
- What a tenant opens affects only its own pods.

The remaining risk is the exfiltration limit described under "Residual risks and limits". A tenant that allows a host accepting uploads gives its agents a channel to that host.

### Relationship to the current pool egress configuration

| Current setting                                                                                                     | Effect of the design                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `egressProfile: restricted`                                                                                         | Kept. `gitAccess` can add `lenny-git-mirror` and `lenny-egress`, limited by the decision to the git hosts of the `gitAccess` formula (see "Effective policy").                                                                                                                                                          |
| `egressProfile: provider-direct`                                                                                    | Removed. See "Consolidating `provider-direct`" below.                                                                                                                                                 |
| `egressProfile: internet`                                                                                           | Kept for unfiltered egress and non-HTTP protocols. `netagent` can still run in these pools for git credentials and audit, with an added NetworkPolicy rule that allows the in-cluster Lenny services. |
| New `egressProfile: proxied`                                                                                        | Pod egress goes only to the base destinations, the `proxied` DNS resolver, `lenny-egress`, `lenny-egress-authz` on the pod binding API, the Token Service on the pod credential API, and `lenny-git-mirror`, each on its port.                                                              |
| `deliveryMode` | Removed with direct delivery mode under LU-4. A pool write that names it is refused with `400 VALIDATION_ERROR` naming the field, the rule the owner set on 2026-11-13 for every removed field (AR O3). |
| The base policy, the object store and OTLP policies, and the `egressCIDRs.excludePrivate` and `excludeIMDS` Helm values | Unchanged. `egressCIDRs.providers` goes with `provider-direct`, and pods with `egressProfile: proxied` get a per-profile DNS resolver address (see "Residual risks and limits", **DNS channel**). |
| §26.2 "coding runtimes default to `restricted` with an allowlist" and the non-existent `unrestricted` value         | Removed. See "Runtime categories in §26.2" below.                                                                                                                                                     |

### Consolidating `provider-direct`

`provider-direct` works as follows today (§13.2.6):

- It exists for `deliveryMode: direct`, where the pod receives a short-lived LLM provider lease and calls the provider itself. NET-006 pairs `direct` with `provider-direct`, and pairs `proxy` with `restricted`, where the gateway calls the provider.
- The chart renders `allow-pod-egress-provider-direct`, with one `ipBlock` per entry of the Helm value `egressCIDRs.providers` on TCP 443, minus the IMDS addresses (`agent-network-policies.yaml`).
- The list is deployment-wide and holds CIDRs only, because NetworkPolicy cannot match names. The default is empty, so a pod with `provider-direct` and no CIDRs reaches nothing beyond the base destinations.

Its weaknesses are that provider CIDR ranges are wide and shared (a cloud provider's ranges include every tenant's endpoints on that cloud), that they change without notice and are maintained by hand, and that one list serves every pool.

Recommendation: remove it now. No deployment depends on it, so no migration path is needed, and LU-4 removes `deliveryMode: direct`, its last consumer, so nothing replaces the pairing. A runtime that calls a provider outside the gateway's LLM proxy reaches it as any other egress traffic, under `egressProfile: proxied` and a pool ceiling that names the provider's host set. The removal touches NET-006 and NET-044; the chart template `agent-network-policies.yaml`, `credentialpool-egress-guard.yaml`, and `admission-policies/direct-mode-isolation-webhook.yaml` with their tests; `egressCIDRs.providers` in `values.yaml`; the admission validators in `pool_config_validator` and `direct_mode_isolation`; the CRD enum and `pkg/embedded/crds`; `pkg/sandbox/egress/profile.go`; `poolstore.go`, `admin/pools.go`, `bootstrap_resources.go`, `sessionserver/credential_preclaim.go`, and `warmpool/controller.go`; preflight; and `tests/tier9_security/pool_admission_isolation_test.go`. LLM traffic through `lenny-egress` is a long-lived streaming CONNECT. The tunnel lifetime cap must exceed the longest LLM stream, or the client retries.

### Runtime categories in §26.2

§26.2 "Shared Patterns for Coding-Agent Runtimes" states that "Coding-agent runtimes default to `egressProfile: restricted` ... HTTPS to the operator-configured allowlist" and that unrestricted egress needs "`egressProfile: unrestricted`". Neither the allowlist nor the value exists. The same section keys other defaults on the coding-agent category: `isolationProfile: sandboxed`, `deliveryMode: proxy`, `setupCommandPolicy`, and `preConnect: false`.

The owner commits of June 2026 (`f79b3b788` and `5b98a6d08`) removed platform enforcement keyed on a coding-agent category, and [`BUILD-GAPS.md`](../BUILD-GAPS.md) records that the §26.1 catalog table "carries a documentation-only \"Category\" column". The egress paragraph and the other category-keyed defaults survived that change. No record of the owner's broader feedback that the specification should not define runtime types and their defaults was found.

The design removes the category-keyed prose from §26.2. Platform defaults apply to every Runtime alike, and §5 and §13.2.6 state them. Each shipped reference runtime documents its own `defaultPoolConfig` descriptively in its §26 entry (for example, "the `claude-code` reference runtime ships `egressProfile: proxied` with the host sets `github`, `npm`, and `pypi`"). This change sits in EG-9, and T-26.2.3 closes with it.

## Effective policy

`lenny-egress-authz` computes the effective policy from the binding:

In these formulas, `ceiling` is the pool's egress ceiling with the tenant's pool overlay applied (see "Tenant pool overlays"). Every pool-level setting therefore applies in every case, including pods with several slots. Apart from the `gitAccess` case, which replaces the ceiling, the cases differ only in which tenant and session inputs apply.

- **Unbound pod** (warm, before any tenant): `ceiling ∩ (pool warm-up policy ∪ warm-list hosts)`, where `ceiling` is the pool's own ceiling, because no overlay applies without a tenant, and `warm-list hosts` is the hosts of the entries on the pod's warm list, the one for its pool and its revision, so a pod warming for revision 7 reaches only the hosts of revision 7's entries.
- **Tenant-bound pod without a session** (a tenant-owned pool's warm pod or a pinned idle pod): `ceiling ∩ (tenant always-allowed ∪ tenant warm-up additions ∪ warm-list hosts) − tenant denied`. A platform entry whose host the tenant denies is left off a tenant-owned pool's warm list at render, so the deny never fails that pool's fills.
- **Pod with one session**: `ceiling ∩ session allowed − tenant denied`, further limited to a binding's hosts and the base destinations when a `userCredential` binding sets `lockEgressToHosts`. A session with that option is refused on a pool with `egressProfile: internet`. The limit persists for every descendant: each records it on its session row as `egressLockHosts`, and a delegated child is refused on a pool with `egressProfile: internet` by the same test. `session allowed` is the session's `egress.allowedHosts` list when the plan sets one, and the tenant's always-allowed hosts otherwise. Each entry of the list must lie within `tenant always-allowed ∪ tenant session-requestable`, or the session is rejected at create.
- **Pool with `egressProfile: restricted` and `gitAccess`**: `gitAccess hosts ∩ git hosts − tenant denied`. A session carrying `egress.allowedHosts`, its own or inherited, intersects this set with that list. `gitAccess hosts` is the pool's list, any host by default, and it takes the ceiling's place because a pool with `egressProfile: restricted` has no general egress. `git hosts` is the hosts of the tenant's VCS pools and explicit git routes, the LFS host sets of those pools' providers, and the hosts of the pod's warm list. An unbound warm pod has no tenant, so its `git hosts` is the hosts of its warm list alone, which on a shared pool holds public platform entries only, and it has no LFS hosts. A tenant's pool overlay that disables `gitAccess` empties the set. The platform default route does not count as a git route, so a pod with `egressProfile: restricted` cannot reach an arbitrary host through it. A `gitClone` whose host lies outside this set is rejected at create. `lockEgressToHosts` limits this set as it does the one-session formula.
- **Pod with several slots**: each slot's session is evaluated by the formula that fits it, the one-session formula or the `gitAccess` formula, on the connections `netagent` attributes to that slot ("Slot attribution and fetch grants", **Per-slot scopes (decision 60)**), and a connection attributed to no session's slot is refused. The slots share one network namespace and one `netagent` ([SI-10](session-isolation-design.md#si-10-what-stays-shared)), and attribution by owner UID is what keeps one slot's grant from another slot's processes. On a pool whose runtime class does not answer attribution, the fallback is `ceiling ∩ tenant always-allowed − tenant denied` for the whole pod, a session or delegated child carrying `egress.allowedHosts` or `egressLockHosts`, its own or inherited, is refused on it with `ENVIRONMENT_POLICY_NOT_ENFORCEABLE` under [`tenant-scoping-identity-design.md`](tenant-scoping-identity-design.md), per-session credential bindings do not apply, so a session naming an `onRequest` binding or attaching a `poolAttach` binding is refused with the same code ("Slot attribution and fetch grants", **Fallback pools.**), and the readable and writable sets are the union over the pod's sessions. The pod is tenant-pinned (§5.2) in both cases.
- **Delegated child session**: a child in the parent's environment inherits the parent's `egress.allowedHosts`, its `egressLockHosts`, and named bindings, except a `userCredential` binding, which a child receives only when the delegation lease, the record of what the child receives from its parent, names it, because a §8.2 `TaskSpec` carries no host list and a child would otherwise fall to the tenant's always-allowed hosts, which can be wider than a parent that narrowed. A child in another environment, reached through a declared sharing path under [`tenant-scoping-identity-design.md` decision 14](tenant-scoping-identity-design.md#decisions), runs under that environment's policy unchanged and takes no input from the parent, except a `userCredential` binding the delegation lease names.

**Where the pool checks run.** The refusals above that depend on the pool run at the post-chain point of [RT-2](runtime-routing-design.md#rt-2-the-interceptor-contract), after the `PreRoute` chain and revision selection and before the pod claim, on the final pool the chain leaves. They are the refusal of `egress.allowedHosts` or `egressLockHosts`, and of an `onRequest` or `poolAttach` binding, on a pod with several slots whose pool's runtime class does not answer slot attribution, the refusal of `lockEgressToHosts` on a pool with `egressProfile: internet`, which [TS-1](tenant-scoping-identity-design.md#ts-1-environment-scoping) also lists among its rules on the final row, the refusal of a `gitClone` source whose host lies outside the `gitAccess` set, and the refusal of a `gitClone` source on a pool with no git path ("Credentials", **The gateway runs no git.**). They run on every creation path, including `kind: delegate` and `kind: derive`, and on a runtime and pool that no interceptor changed. The gateway's resolution of `gitClone` refs runs after these checks on every creation path, because the gateway cause assertion its `ls-refs` request carries names the final pool and E ("Credentials", **The gateway runs no git.**). Where the plan arrives with the create request, resolution also runs before the pod claim. At finalize, the pod was claimed at create under proposal 0007, so a bad ref fails the finalize on a claimed pod, and the assertion takes E from the session row. When the chain changed the runtime or pool, the refusal keeps its own code and names the last modifier in `details.interceptor_ref`, and the gateway does not fall back to the requested pool. The checks that only validate the plan's own lists, such as each `egress.allowedHosts` entry lying within `tenant always-allowed ∪ tenant session-requestable`, do not depend on the pool and run at create as before.

Readable set entries are records with a `state`, an optional `expiresAt`, and an optional `verifiedAt` the mint checks compare when present, like writable entries, in the binding record and feed. A per-VCS-pool input, which [`user-git-identity-design.md`](user-git-identity-design.md) sets for a VCS pool with `userAuthorization: required` under [UG-2](user-git-identity-design.md#ug-2-repository-authorization), can make a pool's readable set declared-only, and its default keeps the rule this paragraph states. Readable repositories are the session's `gitClone` sources plus the tenant's repository allowlist, together with the public platform entries of the pod's warm list. Before a session binds, a pod's readable set is its warm list, for its pool and revision, plus the repositories of the warm-up policy that applies to it: the pool's warm-up policy on an unbound pod, and the tenant's warm-up additions on a tenant-bound pod (decision 45). Writable repositories are described under "Credentials", **Write scope**. The route for each host is the first matching tenant route, then the platform default (`direct`).

### Scenarios

**A coding agent with defaults.** Pool `coding` has `egressProfile: proxied` and the ceiling `npm, pypi, go-proxy, github`. Tenant `acme` allows the same host sets and has a GitHub App pool for `github.com/acme/`. Alice starts a session that clones `acme/web` with write scope.

- `npm ci` and `pip install` reach their registries.
- `curl https://example.com` gets 403, which is logged with tenant `acme` and Alice's session.
- `git push` mints a one-hour token for `acme/web` with `contents: write`, which stays in `netagent`.
- A push to `acme/payments` is refused by `netagent`, because that repository is outside the session's write scope.

**A research agent with open web access.** Pool `research` has `egressProfile: proxied` and the ceiling `*`. Tenant `acme` allows `*` but denies `pastebin.com` and `*.ngrok.io`.

- The agent browses any public site.
- `http://169.254.169.254` and `http://10.0.0.5` get refused by the SSRF filter.
- Every connection is logged per session.

**A regulated tenant behind its corporate gateway.** The platform admin registers upstream `globex-swg`, Globex's security web gateway, and egress group `globex-egress`, whose replicas leave the cluster from a dedicated NAT address. The admin registers that address with Globex's gateway as a trusted location and grants both to tenant `globex`. Globex routes `*` to `globex-swg` through `globex-egress`.

- `lenny-egress` forwards every Globex connection to the `globex-egress` replicas, which chain it to the gateway from the registered address. The gateway identifies Globex by that address.
- Globex's gateway applies its own policy and can intercept TLS. Globex's pods then need the gateway's CA, which the pool's CA trust bundle provides.
- Tenant `acme` on the same pool goes direct.

**A self-hosted GitLab on a private address.** The platform admin registers git endpoint `initech-gitlab` (`https://gitlab.initech.internal`, on a private address with a Helm `ipBlock`) and grants it to tenant `initech`. Initech adds a GitLab credential pool and a git route.

- Pods clone and push through `netagent`, which terminates the host, as the git route covers it, and reaches the endpoint as a raw tunnel through `lenny-egress` to the pinned address.
- Tenant `acme`'s pods cannot reach it. Acme has no route to it, `lenny-egress-authz` refuses the host for Acme, and no pod has a network path to the address. Level 1 replaces that last point with a tenant-scoped policy rendered from Helm values.

**A session that lists its hosts.** Tenant `acme` has always-allowed `npm, pypi` and session-requestable `huggingface.co`. Bob's session sets `egress.allowedHosts: [pypi, huggingface.co]`, so it can download models and Python packages, and `npm` is closed to it. Alice's session sets no list and gets the always-allowed `npm, pypi`, so `huggingface.co` gets 403. A session that lists `example.com` is rejected at create, because that host is neither always-allowed nor session-requestable.

**Warm fill and setup before claim.** Pool `monorepo` has the warm-up policy `npm`, and its Runtime carries a public `gitRepository` entry for `github.com/acme/monorepo`, which puts that repository on the warm list of each of the pool's pod sets. Each warm pod fills its repository store from `lenny-git-mirror` and runs `npm ci` before any session. A tenant-owned pool's private stores come from its warm list, through the `gitRepository` assets of the tenant-derived runtime's `sharedAssets`, which a tenant admin or a platform admin writes, and fill with the tenant's credentials from the pod's creation.

**A coding agent opens a pull request.** Tenant `acme` has a GitHub App credential pool for `github.com/acme/` and a credential binding `acme-github-api`. The binding's hosts are `api.github.com` and `uploads.github.com`, its scope is `always`, its permissions are `pull_requests: write` and `contents: read`, and its delivery is `inject`. Alice's session clones `acme/web` with write scope, and the agent runs `git push origin fix-login` and then `gh pr create`.

- The push mints a `contents: write` token for `acme/web` in `netagent`, as in the first scenario.
- `gh` runs because the profile sets `GH_TOKEN=lenny-injected`. `netagent` terminates TLS for `api.github.com`, mints a token for the session's repositories with the binding's permissions, overwrites `Authorization`, and forwards the request. A mint with any write permission names only repositories with a `granted` writable entry, and a repository the session declared read-only appears only in a read-only mint, so the writable set also limits REST writes through a minted binding.
- `gh pr create --repo acme/payments` gets 404 from GitHub, because the minted token covers only `acme/web`. The host enforces that limit, and `netagent` has no code for GitHub's paths. Because `pull_requests: write` also permits approval as documented, this binding takes the `github-deny` preset by default, which refuses review and merge operations by name as data (see [`github-credential-addon-design.md`, "Preset body"](github-credential-addon-design.md#preset-body)). `gh` reads its host from the unchanged remote, and it needs git in the image even with `-R`.
- The agent can read no token in the pod. The SDK accessor shows the binding, whose placeholder is the profile's constant.

**An agent calls a remote MCP server.** Tenant `globex` stores an API key for a hosted MCP server at `mcp.tracker.example.com`, with the binding `globex-tracker` (scope `onRequest`, delivery `inject`, attach `Authorization: Bearer {{secret}}`). Bob's session names the binding, and the runtime's MCP client connects over streamable HTTP.

- `netagent` attaches the key to each request and streams the server-sent events through the sliding-window scrubber, so tool results arrive as they are sent.
- Carol's session in the same pool does not name the binding. Her requests to the same host carry no key, and the server answers 401.

**A data agent reads S3.** Tenant `initech` creates an IAM OIDC provider for its Lenny issuer (`https://<lenny>/oidc/<initech's tenant ID>`) and a role `initech-analytics` whose trust policy requires Initech's issuer, audience `sts.amazonaws.com`, and subject `lenny:initech:initech-analytics`, with a permissions policy on `s3://initech-analytics/*`. Its binding `initech-analytics` has delivery `federation`, provider template `aws`, and the role ARN. The pool's ceiling includes the `aws-s3` and `aws-sts` host sets. The session runs Python with boto3 and the AWS CLI.

- `netagent` writes the JWT and a config profile naming it on the slot's credentials volume, and the session launch variable `AWS_CONFIG_FILE` points boto3 at the file. boto3 calls `AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity` through `lenny-egress` and receives one-hour credentials for the role.
- SigV4 signing happens in the client, and `lenny-egress` sees CONNECTs to STS and to `initech-analytics.s3.us-east-1.amazonaws.com`.
- The agent can read the JWT. That is why `federation` needs the `agentReadableDelivery` grant and the tenant opt-in. The trust policy limits the token to one role, and the role to one bucket.
- Tenant `globex`, which cannot create an OIDC provider in its AWS account, stores a long-lived access key in a credential pool and uses `metadata` delivery instead. The Token Service mints one-hour session credentials from the key, and `netagent` serves them on the AWS container credentials endpoint.

**A private package registry.** Tenant `initech` mirrors npm and PyPI through `initech.jfrog.io`. It writes two tenant client config files, an `.npmrc` with `registry=https://initech.jfrog.io/artifactory/api/npm/npm/` and a `pip.conf` with the matching `index-url`, neither holding a credential. It adds a binding with hosts `initech.jfrog.io`, `attach: Authorization: Basic {{base64 "svc-agent" ":" secret}}`, and delivery `inject`.

- `npm ci` and `pip install` send unauthenticated requests, and `netagent` attaches Basic authentication on the way out. Round 3 showed both tools installing through this path.
- The tenant's always-allowed hosts list `initech.jfrog.io` and omit `registry.npmjs.org`, so a dependency that bypasses the mirror fails with 403.

**A merge request on self-managed GitLab.** Tenant `initech` uses the private GitLab endpoint from the earlier scenario, with a GitLab project access token that the Token Service mints for each session and revokes at session end. Its binding for the GitLab API uses delivery `inject` with TLS termination.

- The pool's profile sets `GITLAB_HOST=gitlab.initech.internal` and `GITLAB_TOKEN=lenny-injected`. `glab mr create` sends HTTPS through `HTTPS_PROXY` and trusts the per-pod CA through `SSL_CERT_FILE`, and `netagent` overwrites the token header and forwards the request. Round 3 confirmed that `glab` honours all of these variables. The plain-HTTP option (`api_protocol: http`) is not usable here, because it lives in `glab`'s config file, which `glab` accepts only at mode 0600.
- The token is a `mint-coarse` token, wider than one repository. `netagent` path enforcement limits git use to the session's repositories, and the API calls rely on the project token's own scope and on the `gitlab-deny` preset, resolved from the pool's provider. `glab` reads its host from the unchanged remote.

**A user's own token.** Tenant `acme` defines a user credential type `jira`, and its backend registers Alice's token. A `userCredential` binding on `acme.atlassian.net` delivers it to her session by `inject`, with no gate, or by `file`, with the `userCredentialDelivery` grant and a tenant opt-in. Carol's session in the same pool receives Carol's token or nothing. Pods that can serve another session or a later session are refused. [`user-credential-delivery-design.md`](user-credential-delivery-design.md) traces this, including what `file` does not promise.

**A chat runtime calls a tenant API.** Pool `assistant` runs a chat runtime with two slots per pod. Tenant `globex` has a CRM on a private address. The platform admin registers it as a private service with an `ipBlock` and grants it to `globex`, and Globex adds a binding with scope `always`.

- Every `globex` session on the pool reaches the CRM with the binding attached, and `lenny-egress` dials the address the decision pins.
- A session that names an `onRequest` binding receives it on its own slot alone, because `netagent` attributes each connection to its slot (decision 60). On a runtime class where attribution does not answer, per-session bindings do not apply on the pool, and the `always` binding still does.

**An air-gapped deployment.** The cluster has no direct internet route. The platform admin sets the platform default route to upstream `corp-proxy` for every tenant, registers internal mirrors as host sets (`npm-internal`, `pypi-internal`, and `git-internal`), and sets pool ceilings to those host sets.

- `lenny-egress` chains every connection to `corp-proxy`. The Envoy SSRF filter and the decision service still apply first.
- The mirror's warm fills come from `git-internal` through the same path.
- The `lenny-egress` NetworkPolicy allows only the proxy's address, so a misconfigured direct route fails closed.

**A single-tenant deployment on an existing Squid.** Acme's policy forbids adding a proxy product, and its Squid cannot call a decision service. The platform admin sets `centralProxy.contract: level1` with the acknowledgement, and a sync job renders the policy document into the Squid configuration every minute.

- Preflight fails until Squid serves TLS and requires credentials, and warm pods do not fill until the post-install probe shows that Squid refuses private destinations.
- A pool such as `coding` has one level 1 identity per tenant. A session's `egress.allowedHosts` narrows what `netagent` allows, and Squid enforces the pool's whole list.
- The platform admin cannot register an upstream proxy or an egress group, and the validators name level 1 as the reason.

**Long LLM streams outside the LLM proxy.** Pool `agents` uses `egressProfile: proxied` with a ceiling that includes the `anthropic` host set, and the tenant's binding with `delivery: inject` on that host carries the tenant's own provider key, an ordinary binding credential under LU-4's removal of direct delivery mode. The runtime calls the provider itself rather than the gateway's LLM proxy and streams completions that last up to 40 minutes.

- Each completion is a CONNECT tunnel to the provider through `lenny-egress`, decided and logged per session.
- The pool names the 2-hour entry of the tunnel lifetime menu, above the longest stream. A cap below it would end streams mid-completion, and the client would retry.
- When the session ends, `netagent` closes the session's tunnels at once, before the cap.

## Injected configuration

Tools find the local endpoint through configuration. No formal standard defines proxy configuration for command-line tools:

- `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, and `NO_PROXY` are a convention that curl started. Most HTTP libraries follow it, with differences in letter case, leading dots, and CIDR support.
- PAC files and WPAD are standards for browsers, and command-line tools ignore them.
- The trust variables (`SSL_CERT_FILE` and the per-ecosystem variables listed under "TLS interception") are conventions of the same kind.

The design layers the configuration so that Lenny maintains only these conventions, and tool-specific knowledge stays with the runtime image or the deployer.

**1. Fixed endpoints.** In every pod, the local egress endpoint is `127.0.0.1:3128`, and the directory `netagent` writes is `/run/netagent`. A deployment can change the port. Because these values are constants, a runtime image can carry tool configuration that names them, baked in at build time, such as a Maven `settings.xml`. Files baked into the image are available at launch. Image `ENV` is not, because the supervisor starts the runtime from an empty environment (§4.7.10, **Environment**).

**2. Convention variables at launch.** The controller adds the proxy and trust variables to the runtime's launch environment, as supervisor `--env` pairs next to `LENNY_ADAPTER_SOCKET` on every runtime container. The values are not secret, so rendering them as arguments is acceptable. A Runtime `env` entry that names one of them is rejected for pools with `egressProfile: proxied`. Commands the agent runs inherit the variables from the runtime process, unless the runtime builds an empty environment for them. The default set is:

- `HTTPS_PROXY`, `https_proxy`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `http_proxy`, `ALL_PROXY`, `all_proxy`, `NO_PROXY`, and `no_proxy`. Both cases are set, because curl reads only lowercase `http_proxy`. The controller derives `NO_PROXY` from the base destinations. Today it receives only the gateway gRPC address; the chart therefore gains one Helm value for the LLM proxy host, consumed by both the gateway's `--llm-proxy-public-url` flag (which no template renders today) and the controller, and passes it with the OTLP collector host to the controller as inputs on the same path as `GatewayGRPCAddr`, and the controller renders all three into `NO_PROXY`, so platform traffic from the runtime is never sent to `netagent`, where the address filter would refuse it. On pools with `egressProfile: proxied` that value must be an in-cluster Service name: the agent NetworkPolicy admits LLM proxy traffic to gateway pods by selector only, and the `proxied` DNS resolver answers no public name, so a public hairpin URL would fail twice. Preflight checks the value, and the same rule applies to the OTLP host. Presigned object-store hosts are not enumerable and are used by the adapter only, which bypasses the proxy explicitly (see "Runtime SDKs", **The adapter's own transports**). `netagent` receives the same list over the pod binding API and logs a CONNECT for one of those hosts as a configuration error instead of tunnelling it. `NO_PROXY` lists exact, fully qualified names only: .NET and Go read leading dots differently, curl, Node, and Go do not match a short Service name against `.svc`, and Node ignores CIDR entries. `ALL_PROXY` is always `http://`, because a `socks5://` value makes curl resolve names locally.
- Switches that are settings rather than files:
  - `NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY=1`, which needs Node 22.21 or 24.5 and later.
  - `npm_config_proxy`, `npm_config_https_proxy`, and `npm_config_noproxy`.
  - `YARN_HTTP_PROXY` and `YARN_HTTPS_PROXY`. `YARN_NO_PROXY` is never set.
  - `CARGO_HTTP_PROXY` and `CARGO_NET_GIT_FETCH_WITH_CLI=true`.
  - `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS` with the JVM proxy properties.
  - `GLAB_CHECK_UPDATE=false` and `GLAB_SEND_TELEMETRY=false`.
- The trust variables, pointing at `/run/netagent/ca-bundle.pem`. That bundle holds the chart's public root set, any deployer CA, and the per-pod CA. `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` points at the added CAs only, and npm needs it because npm ignores `SSL_CERT_FILE`.
- `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM=/run/netagent/gitconfig`. Lenny's rules take the system scope, which is read-only by convention, so the agent's `git config --global` keeps working against its writable home and an image-baked `~/.gitconfig` still applies. The rendered file starts with `[include] path = /etc/gitconfig`, which git ignores when the file is absent, so an image that ships a system config (for example with `safe.directory`) keeps it, and Lenny's rules follow it. `NETRC=/run/netagent/credentials/netrc` on every pod, a path on the slot's own credentials volume whose file is empty when the session has no `netrc` projection, `LENNY_NETWORK_DIR=/run/netagent`, `LENNY_CREDENTIALS_DIR`, and the `inject` placeholders, which are the profile's constant (for example `GH_TOKEN=lenny-injected`).
- The pointer variables for tenant client config files at their fixed paths: `NPM_CONFIG_USERCONFIG=/run/netagent/config/npmrc` and `PIP_CONFIG_FILE=/run/netagent/config/pip.conf`. They are constants, so they can be launch variables, and a tenant file at another name needs a pointer from the runtime image or a deployer profile.

**Harness clients.** The harness-egress spike of 2026-11-15 ran Claude Code 2.1.323, Codex CLI 0.136.0, the TypeScript MCP SDK on Node 22, and the Python MCP SDK against an HTTP MCP server through a stand-in for `netagent` that terminated TLS with a per-pod CA, and found no variable missing from this profile. Its per-client table, in [`evidence/connector-removal-design/harness-egress-spike/RESULTS.md`](evidence/connector-removal-design/harness-egress-spike/RESULTS.md) and restated in [`connector-removal-design.md`, "CR-3 External services"](connector-removal-design.md#cr-3-external-services), **Proxy and trust.**, is the evidence for the proxy and trust variables above **[measured]**:

- Claude Code honours `HTTPS_PROXY`, `https_proxy`, `HTTP_PROXY`, and `NO_PROXY`, trusts `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`, and ignores `SSL_CERT_FILE`.
- Codex honours `HTTPS_PROXY`, `https_proxy`, `NO_PROXY`, and `no_proxy`, and does not use `HTTP_PROXY` for https URLs, so `HTTPS_PROXY` is the variable that must be set for it. It trusts `SSL_CERT_FILE` and `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE` and ignores `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`.
- The TypeScript MCP SDK honours the proxy only with `NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY=1` or `--use-env-proxy`, and trusts `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` alone. The Python MCP SDK honours `HTTPS_PROXY` and `NO_PROXY` by default and trusts `SSL_CERT_FILE`, ignoring `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`.

Claude Code reads only `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`, and Codex reads only `SSL_CERT_FILE` or `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE`, so both `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` and `SSL_CERT_FILE` stay in the profile. `SSL_CERT_FILE` replaces the trust store for Codex and for Python, which is why it names the whole pod trust bundle, public roots included. `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE` is optional and the profile does not set it, because Codex falls back to `SSL_CERT_FILE` when it is unset, as the Codex authentication page that the removal design's "Sources" lists states. The run was on loopback with a Go stand-in, and a run in a pod on runc, gVisor, and Kata stays owed ("Validation still owed").

Every variable of this layer is the same for every session the pod serves. A value that depends on the session, such as the variable naming a `federation` binding's client configuration file or Azure's identifiers, is a session launch variable: `netagent` writes it into the session's binding list on `slot-<n>-netcred`, and the supervisor adds it to the runtime's launch environment when it launches the session's runtime process, after the adapter's wait for the applied coordination generation. A session launch variable never names a variable this layer sets.

**3. The network descriptor.** `netagent` writes `/run/netagent/network.json` for runtimes that configure clients in code, such as a headless browser, aiohttp without `trust_env`, or an MCP client. It holds:

- the endpoints and the CA bundle path;
- the `NO_PROXY` list;
- the launch variables of layer 2, which are the same for every session;
- a pointer to the session's binding list, `/run/netagent/credentials/bindings.json`.

The descriptor names no session and holds no per-session value. The binding list holds the session's credential bindings with their name, hosts, delivery, and file path, the session launch variables, and the coordination generation of the binding `netagent` has applied for the slot. `netagent` writes it to the slot's own credentials volume, `slot-<n>-netcred`, so a slot reads its own session's bindings and no other slot's, as "Delivery modes" states for `delivery: file` values. `netagent` rewrites each binding list atomically whenever its session's binding changes, rewrites the descriptor when a pod-wide value changes, and empties the binding list at the slot's release.

**4. Extensions.** A platform admin replaces or extends the launch variables per deployment, Runtime, or pool, and adds file templates for tools the conventions miss. Tenant client config files (for example an `.npmrc` that names a private registry) are written verbatim per tenant, with no templating. Lenny ships no tool-specific file templates. The runtime author guide documents the gaps round 3 found and how an image or a profile closes them:

- Maven 3.9 and 4.0 ignore the JVM proxy properties, because their default Apache transport does not read them. An image ships `settings.xml` with a `<proxies>` entry naming `127.0.0.1:3128`, and `MAVEN_ARGS=-s <path>` points Maven at it, or the deployer adds the same file as a template.
- Gradle 8 and 9 honour `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS`, `GRADLE_OPTS`, and `systemProp.*` lines in `gradle.properties`, for dependencies and for the wrapper download, and need nothing more.
- System-wide files are out of reach, because the root filesystem is read-only and the pod runs as non-root. A runtime that needs `apt` bakes its proxy config into the image.

**What `netagent` writes, in total**: the pod-wide git config, the CA bundle, the descriptor, each slot's binding list, git config file, and commit template, the `netrc` file, the credential directories for bindings with `delivery: file` and their projections, the token and client configuration files for bindings with `delivery: federation`, the token file for a binding with `delivery: metadata`, the profile's file templates, and the tenant's client config files. It writes nothing else.

Tools that ignore every proxy setting fail to connect, because the NetworkPolicy allows no direct route, so they fail closed. The runtime author guide lists the known cases: aiohttp without `trust_env`, and headless browsers without an explicit proxy option.

## Runtime SDKs

### Current state

The runtime SDKs in Go, Python, and TypeScript (`sdks/runtime/`) are protocol codecs. Each implements the following:

- the frame loop and multiplexing by `sessionId`;
- the dial of the platform MCP socket, which a runtime that starts child sessions or calls platform tools uses;
- CH-RUNTIMEOPS, which a runtime that declares `interrupt` or `checkpoint` uses;
- reading the credential file that `session_start.credentialsPath` names, and rebinding on `credentials_rotated`.

[`agent-runtimes-only-design.md`](agent-runtimes-only-design.md) removes the multiplexing, because no frame other than `session_start` carries `sessionId`, and LU-4 removes the LLM credential file and `credentials_rotated`.

None of them builds an environment, spawns subprocesses, reads proxy variables or a CA bundle, or helps with git. The supervisor builds the runtime's environment at launch, and commands the agent runs inherit it. A runtime may implement the protocol without any SDK (§26 preamble). [`runtime-protocol-design.md`](runtime-protocol-design.md#rp-2-capabilities-and-their-dependencies) replaces the integration levels with declared capabilities, moves platform tool calls onto frames on `CH-MSGSOCK` in RP-B (connector tool calls left with connectors under [`connector-removal-design.md`](connector-removal-design.md)), and decides under its O7 whether `CH-RUNTIMEOPS` stays. None of that changes what this design adds to the runtime's environment.

### Design rule

Every mechanism in this design works with no runtime code:

- the launch variables;
- the git config, named by `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM`;
- `inject` in `netagent`;
- `federation` and `metadata` through the cloud SDKs' own provider chains;
- `file` in a fixed directory.

A runtime without an SDK, and a shell command the agent runs, never pass through SDK code. A mechanism that depended on SDK code would fail without notice for them, which would make the design brittle. The SDKs therefore add only conveniences that read what the platform already provides.

### What the SDKs add

The SDKs add one read-only accessor over the network descriptor: `NetworkFrom(ctx)` in Go, `tools.network` in Python, and `ctx.network` in TypeScript. It returns the following:

- The endpoints, the CA bundle path, and the `NO_PROXY` list, for clients built in code.
- `env()`: the launch variables of layer 2 and the session launch variables as a map, for a runtime that builds a clean environment for the commands it spawns and must keep the proxy and trust variables in it.
- `bindings()`: the session's credential bindings, with name, hosts, and delivery, and the profile's constant placeholder for a binding with `delivery: inject`.
- `credential(name)`: the current value of a binding with `delivery: file`, read from disk on every call so that rotation needs no watcher.

The accessor reads the descriptor and the slot's binding list, and it retries for up to a bounded time when the binding list is not yet written. This covers the short gap between the binding and `netagent`'s rewrite. Its size per SDK is a few dozen lines, with tests that fix the descriptor schema.

### What the SDKs do not add

- No proxy, TLS, minting, or token handling. `netagent` owns all of it.
- No tool configuration, template engine, or file watcher.
- No git helpers. `git-credential-lenny` and `lenny/vcs_token` are removed, and git reaches `netagent` through the git config.
- No new frames, channels, or `session_start` fields. The descriptor is a file, and CH-MSGSOCK and CH-RUNTIMEOPS are unchanged.

### The adapter's own transports

The adapter's transports read the proxy environment today: `pkg/adapter/checkpointtransport.go` clones `http.DefaultTransport`, which uses `ProxyFromEnvironment`, and the gateway gRPC client sets no proxy option. Presigned object-store URLs carry bucket names as hosts that `NO_PROXY` cannot enumerate. The adapter therefore disables proxying explicitly on every platform transport (`Proxy: nil` on its HTTP transports and `WithNoProxy` on the gateway gRPC client), and a tier 2 test checks that checkpoint uploads and the gateway link reach their endpoints directly under the launch variables. The adapter container receives none of the launch variables, so this is a second line.

### Runtimes without an SDK, and commands the agent runs

These read the environment and the files directly, and they need nothing else. A runtime that strips the environment of the commands it spawns must keep the proxy and trust variables. The runtime author guide states this, and the `env()` accessor gives SDK runtimes the list.

### SDK gaps found during the review

The review found these existing gaps, which are outside this design and should be filed as findings:

- §15.7 names the Go module `github.com/lennylabs/runtime-sdk-go` and the Apache-2.0 licence. The tree uses the root-module path and the MIT licence, and the scaffolder templates import the spec's path.
- §15.7 promises SIGTERM handling, a wrapper for the lease-refresh loop, workspace utilities, telemetry, and a test server. None of them exists.
- No SDK populates `CreateRequest.WorkspacePlan`.
- The session `env` that §14 says is "injected into the agent session" has no delivery path to a shared runtime process.
- No SDK helper acts on `llm.apiKeyEnv`.
- The Python scaffold's Dockerfile sets `ENV PATH`, which the supervisor drops at launch.
- `git-credential-lenny` reads the current manifest nonce, the value in the adapter manifest (`/run/lenny/adapter-manifest.json`) that authenticates a connection to the pod's intra-pod MCP servers, so it was fragile on recycled pods whose runtime process was kept and unusable on concurrent pods. This design removes it.

## Pod models

| Pod model                                         | Identity before claim | Policy                                                                              | Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| :------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| One session per slot (default)                    | Service account token | Pool warm-up policy, then the session policy after claim                            | Warm fills, shared assets, and the warm-up checkout use the warm-up policy. The runtime process launches at bind, after materialization and setup, so it never runs under the warm-up policy, and the per-pod CA can always carry name constraints for it ("Delivery modes"). |
| One session per pod (`maxConcurrentSessionsPerPod: 1`) | Service account token | Per session                                                                    |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Sequential reuse (recycle)                        | Service account token | Per session. Between sessions, the pinned idle pod uses the tenant warm-up policy.  | `netagent` closes the previous session's tunnels when the binding changes. The slot release ends the session's runtime process, sweeps the slot, and stages the next warm-up checkout under the tenant warm-up policy ([SI-3](session-isolation-design.md#si-3-runtime-processes-slot-release-and-setup-commands)). |
| Concurrent slots (`maxConcurrentSessionsPerPod` above 1) | Service account token | Per session, on the connections `netagent` attributes to each slot (decision 60); per pool and tenant where attribution does not answer | Each session runs in its own slot container under its own UID, and the slots share the network namespace and `netagent`. `netagent` attributes each connection to its slot ("Slot attribution and fetch grants") and closes a session's tunnels at its slot's release. The deployer acknowledges the shared pod with `acknowledgeSharedPod`. |
| Tenant-owned pool (private warm-list entries) | Service account token | Tenant warm-up policy from warm-up                                                  | The pool's ownership binds every warm pod to the tenant before any session. The controller stamps `lenny.dev/tenant-id` with the owner at pod creation, which needs a new `unset → owner` rule for the controller in the label webhook (§5.2, staged in proposal 2), so egress-group admission and the tenant warm-up additions apply from the first second. `ownerTenantId` can be set only when the pool is created and is immutable afterwards: adding an owner to a running pool would leave warm pods with no owner label, and a change would leave existing pods labelled with the old owner, which the gateway's re-stamp cannot correct and the tenant pin check (`tenantpin`) would refuse. Making a pool tenant-owned or changing its owner therefore means creating a pool under a new name and draining the old one. A deleted pool's name is not reusable, because the pool table's primary key is the name (`sandbox_warm_pools.name`) and deletion is soft, and that also keeps a claim from admitting an old pod. A claim selects by pool name and, under [RV-3](runtime-revisions-design.md#rv-3-pods), by the revision of the pod set it claims from, and an old pod under a reused name would match the pool name. Pool deletion cascades to its overlays, grants, and level 1 identities, and the claim-time tenant pin admission (`AdmitTenantPin`) refuses an unlabelled pod on a tenant-owned pool. A label stamped at creation conflicts with that admission and with §5.2 **Pinned idle inventory**, which read the label as "has served a session", so a non-recycling owned pool would drain its own warm pods. EG-9 therefore adopts `lenny.dev/served`, stamped at first assignment, and both readers use it on a tenant-owned pool, as [`tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`](tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md) states. |

Only an unbound warm pod is tenant-agnostic until claim. Pinned idle pods and the pods of tenant-owned pools are bound to a tenant before a session exists, and the policy table covers each case. Every pod model is an agent pod running sessions, because [`agent-runtimes-only-design.md`](agent-runtimes-only-design.md) removes service mode and `type: mcp` runtimes.

## Network policy and placement

A new `egressProfile: proxied` value allows the base destinations (gateway, object store, and OTLP when configured), the `proxied` DNS resolver, the `lenny-egress` pods, the `lenny-egress-authz` pods for the pod binding API, the Token Service's pod credential API port, and the `lenny-git-mirror` pods, selected by pod label. A deployer git service running in the cluster gets no pod-side rule, and pods reach it through the central proxy like any other private destination, because a label-selected rule would be a raw path for every tenant. Policy evaluates traffic after Service NAT and pod IPs are unstable, so an `ipBlock` cannot name such a service. Its registration therefore carries a namespace and pod selector, which the chart renders as a peer on the `lenny-egress` policy, and the decision pins its Service address. A pool with `egressProfile: restricted` and `gitAccess` adds the `lenny-git-mirror` and `lenny-egress` peers, with the decision limited to the git hosts of the `gitAccess` formula under "Effective policy" and no label-selected rule to any deployer git service. Pools with `egressProfile: restricted` and `gitAccess`, and pools with `egressProfile: internet` that run `netagent`, add the `lenny-egress-authz` and Token Service rules for the same reason. Rules that match a Service IP do not work. The controller labels pods at creation, the gateway stamps `lenny.dev/tenant-id` at the first slot reservation (`podclaim/slotclaimer.go`), the controller stamps it with the owner at creation on tenant-owned pools, and Helm pre-creates every policy (§13.2.6), including the egress-group policies below. The profile is tenant-agnostic, and it needs no isolation profile beyond what `egressProfile: restricted` needs, because pods reach only Lenny services. The `lenny-label-immutability` webhook gains the new value.

`lenny-egress`, `lenny-egress-authz`, `lenny-evidence`, and `lenny-git-mirror` run centrally in `lenny-system`. A per-node DaemonSet is rejected for these reasons:

- The mirror holds private repository contents for several tenants, and the decision service holds the policy for all of them. A container escape on an agent node would reach them on every node that runs agent pods, and an agent node hosts several tenants unless the operator gives a tenant dedicated node tenancy, which is set per tenant (`placement.nodeTenancy: dedicated`, [TSS-6](tenant-security-settings-design.md#tss-6-placement-settings) and [PM-2](placement-design.md#pm-2-node-tenancy)).
- Agent nodes carry their node class's taint, which keeps the workloads of other members off them ([PM-1](placement-design.md#pm-1-node-classes-and-presets)). A DaemonSet would need a toleration of every agent class's taint, which admission allows only for the named node agents of [PM-5](placement-design.md#pm-5-rendering), and on the `microvm` class it costs a microVM per node.
- A DaemonSet reserves capacity on every agent node, which lowers warm-pod density. Its local outage drops egress for that node with no failover, and its rolling updates break in-flight tunnels on every node.
- Corporate proxies are central, so a central default keeps replacement a matter of changing an address.

The mirror parses hostile input, and with `hostUsers` at its default a compromised parent that also escapes the container through a kernel flaw can land as root on the node, where it could read the Secrets mounted there. The mirror therefore carries required pod anti-affinity against the Token Service and the decision service, so an escape does not land beside the components that hold provider credentials or policy. The rule is symmetric in the scheduler, so neither can be placed on a node already running the mirror. Other workloads on the mirror's node remain exposed to that chain, which `hostUsers: false` addresses. A deployment needs enough nodes to separate them. [PM-8](placement-design.md#pm-8-system-components) states the mirror's replicas and disruption budget, renders each required anti-affinity pair on both components, and states the `global.devMode` relaxation of every required anti-affinity term to preferred, which lets a single-node cluster schedule the mirror. `gitMirror.hostUsers`, the `lenny-mirror` namespace with its `baseline` Pod Security level, and this rationale stay with this design.

These components run on the system node class, which [PM-1](placement-design.md#pm-1-node-classes-and-presets) defines, and [PM-8](placement-design.md#pm-8-system-components) states their namespaces, priority classes, replicas, disruption budgets, anti-affinity, and spread. This design adds §13.2.4 `lenny-system` NetworkPolicy rows for them. An egress group whose traffic leaves from a dedicated node pool selects that node pool within the system class through `placement.componentPlacement` ([PM-5](placement-design.md#pm-5-rendering)).

**The `proxied` DNS resolver.** It is a second CoreDNS instance beside the chart's dedicated agent DNS, `lenny-agent-dns`. `lenny-agent-dns` serves every other agent pod as §13.2 states, external names included, while the `proxied` resolver answers cluster names alone and returns NXDOMAIN for every other name, which pods with `egressProfile: proxied` need because their clients send the host name inside CONNECT ("Residual risks and limits", **DNS channel.**). The chart renders it as a Deployment, `lenny-proxied-dns` **[design]**, in `lenny-system` behind a Service with a fixed ClusterIP, because the controller writes that address into each pod's `dnsConfig` at creation. It runs 2 replicas with a PodDisruptionBudget of `minAvailable: 1`, at the `system` priority, as PM-8's component table lists it.

Native sidecars count toward the pod's effective request (the sum of the sidecars plus the largest of the regular containers is not used; a sidecar with `restartPolicy: Always` adds its request to the pod's), so a `netagent` request of, for example, 50m and 64Mi raises each pod's footprint by that amount and lowers warm density accordingly. Round 10 measured a Go prototype, and that request suits an ordinary session: idle at 13.5 MiB, and five LLM streams at 21m. A package install needs burst, because 50 new connections per second used 135m. It is a request and never a limit: under a 50m hard limit, 20 new intercepted connections per second gave a 68 s median with 40 of 300 failed, and intercepted throughput fell to 7 to 9 MB/s, against 62 to 68 MB/s at 250m and 652 to 678 MB/s at 1 CPU. In round 12, a hard 64 MiB limit held 400 idle opaque or intercepted HTTP/1.1 tunnels with no margin, 900 with HTTP/2 on both sides, and 250 with gzip decoding, on a prototype. `netagent` sets no `GOMEMLIMIT` near its limit, because a soft limit there cost 0.6 to 2.4 CPUs on idle tunnels, and outcomes stopped being monotonic: at 50 MiB, 600 and 800 tunnels died while 1,000 held. The chart sizes from 256 KiB per tunnel plus the idle footprint. Separately, 20 stalled readers reached 142 to 177 MiB with default buffers and were killed under a 64 MiB limit. `netagent` therefore bounds its socket and stream buffers to 256 KiB, which held the same load at 24 to 36 MiB, and the chart sets a memory limit that fits the pool's tunnel quota. The §6.4 memory bound accounts for it.

The CNIs checked match pod-to-ClusterIP traffic against the backend pod: Calico (Tigera documentation), GKE Dataplane V2, Cilium, and kube-network-policies. AWS VPC CNI does so by translating Service selectors. AWS VPC CNI also needs `NETWORK_POLICY_ENFORCING_MODE=strict`, or a new pod starts with no restrictions. The requirement already holds for `restricted`, but nothing checks it today: §13.2.1 recommends Calico in policy-only mode on VPC CNI, and `pkg/preflight` has no VPC CNI check. Preflight gains one.

Round 3 confirmed from source that Calico's eBPF dataplane and Cilium evaluate egress policy against the post-NAT backend (`felix/bpf-gpl/tc.c` and `bpf/bpf_lxc.c`). On kind, kindnet enforced a `podSelector` egress policy through a ClusterIP and blocked other Services, pod IPs, the default CoreDNS, and the internet. It did not block the API server or the kubelet on the pod's own node, because traffic delivered to the local node bypasses its hook, and its queue rule fails open when its userspace program is absent. kindnet is a test CNI. The Lenny kind tiers must not treat it as evidence of production enforcement, and the conformance tests for `proxied` run against Calico or Cilium.

Round 5 ran the `proxied` policy (default-deny egress plus `podSelector` peers for the proxy, the `proxied` DNS resolver, and the gateway, with no `ipBlock`) on two-node kind clusters with Calico 3.33 in its iptables and eBPF dataplanes and with Cilium 1.20 with kube-proxy replacement. On every dataplane, all allowed paths worked through ClusterIP Services, including a backend on the client's own node, and every denied destination was dropped: the API server ClusterIP, both nodes' kubelet ports, hostNetwork pods on the same and the other node, a pod in another namespace, a Service fronting that pod (which confirms post-DNAT evaluation), the default kube-dns, and the internet. Calico's iptables dataplane evaluates workload egress policy on the INPUT path as well as FORWARD, and Calico eBPF and Cilium attach policy at the pod's interface, which is why they catch node-local traffic that kindnet's postrouting hook misses. Two ordering points from that run carry into the chart: the `proxied` DNS resolver's ClusterIP must be known when the controller renders a pod's `dnsConfig`, so the chart fixes the Service's address; and the policies were applied to a running client, so the tier 5 tests also cover a pod created before its policies exist.

## Defaults, replacement, and augmentation

Every default can be replaced, and the egress and git defaults can also be augmented.

| Mode                    | What runs                                                                                                              | What the deployer configures                                                                                                                |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Default                 | `netagent`, `lenny-egress` (Envoy), `lenny-egress-authz`, `lenny-git-mirror`, and the shipped host sets                | `egressProfile: proxied` on pools                                                                                                           |
| Augment egress          | The default, with `lenny-egress` chaining selected destinations or tenants to deployer upstream proxies (upstream chaining)           | Upstream registrations and assignments, `ipBlock` entries for private upstreams, and a stable egress address registered with the upstream vendor |
| Replace egress, level 2 | A deployer proxy that calls the egress decision API (Envoy, Squid with the Lenny helper, or a Smokescreen-based proxy with a Lenny decider), which must serve TLS on the proxy hop and must dial pinned addresses to serve private destinations | The proxy, its egress decision API integration, and its address in Helm values                                                                               |
| Replace egress, level 1 (not recommended) | A deployer proxy with no Lenny integration, which enforces a published host list per identity. `netagent` and `lenny-egress-authz` still run, and Envoy does not. | The proxy, a sync job for the policy document, the proxy contract, and the acknowledgement described under "Level 1 replacement" |
| Augment git             | `lenny-git-mirror` for some hosts and deployer git services for others                                                 | Git endpoint registrations and tenant git routes                                                                                            |
| Replace git             | No mirror. Each host is reached through a deployer git service or directly through egress.                             | Git endpoint registrations, or none                                                                                                         |

Corporate security gateways fit the augment mode better than replacement. None of the gateways surveyed documents per-workload proxy Basic authentication. Round 3 also found that none documents reading identity headers from a downstream proxy on an undecrypted CONNECT:

- Netskope requires the downstream proxy to insert `X-Forwarded-For` or `X-Authenticated-User` inside the decrypted TLS session.
- Prisma Access accepts `X-Authenticated-User` only from a trusted source address and needs decryption for header identity. Without decryption, it maps users by known source address.
- Forcepoint reads `X-Authenticated-User` from a downstream proxy, which must decrypt HTTPS.
- Cisco Umbrella maps `X-Forwarded-For` to network identities and registers the downstream proxy's egress address. No user header is documented.
- Zscaler uses `X-Forwarded-For` for sub-locations, and no documentation of reading a downstream `X-Authenticated-User` was found. Its help pages could not be read directly, so this rests on search snippets.
- Cloudflare Gateway proxy endpoints identify by source address or by an Access login with TLS inspection.
- Broadcom Cloud SWG and Skyhigh have no usable documentation on this point.

`lenny-egress` does not decrypt, so the documented identity that works without decryption is the source address. The design therefore identifies tenants to a corporate gateway by **egress groups**:

- An egress group is an additional `lenny-egress` Deployment that the chart renders with the same configuration and the same decision service, placed so that its traffic leaves the cluster from a dedicated, stable address. The platform provides the address with cloud NAT, a CNI egress gateway (Cilium, Calico, or OpenShift EgressIP), or a dedicated node pool. There is no second proxy tier and no new contract: the group's Envoy is `lenny-egress` under another Service name.
- A group is infrastructure, so the group itself is a Helm value, and the chart renders its Deployment and Service. A SaaS operator can pre-provision a pool of groups, each with its own address. Assigning a group to a tenant is admin API data, because a Helm rollout per customer does not scale. The gateway stamps `lenny.dev/egress-group` on a pod at bind, and the controller at creation on a tenant-owned pool, from the assignment at that moment. A new decider in the label webhook lets only those two set it, and only from unset, and it checks map presence, so an empty value is refused. It is a separate rule from the immutable list, which refuses unset to value. The webhook's rule must also cover `pods/status`, which carries labels and which it does not cover today (F-13.2.28), for this label and for `lenny.dev/tenant-id`. From the group's name the chart renders an ingress NetworkPolicy on the group's pods that admits only pods with that label. No Lenny component writes NetworkPolicies, and the rule for pods with `egressProfile: proxied` keeps selecting every `lenny-egress` pod, so the group's ingress policy is what restricts it at the network layer.
- Each group's bootstrap adds a fixed `x-lenny-egress-group` header to every ext_authz check, and the decision service compares it with the pod's stamped label and refuses a mismatch in both directions: an unstamped or differently stamped pod at a group's Envoy, and a stamped pod at the default Envoy. It also refuses a pod whose tenant no longer holds that group. Without the second refusal, a tenant could leave the cluster from another tenant's registered address, which every service that trusts that address (a corporate gateway's trusted location, an address-allowlisted service, or an `aws:SourceIp` condition) would read as that tenant.
- The group's ingress policy also admits the gateway, mirror, and Token Service pods, so the gateway's `ls-refs` requests, the mirror's fetches, and the Token Service's mints for a granted tenant can use the group, as the decision requires of them. Warm pods of a shared pool carry no tenant label and use the default Envoy under the pool warm-up policy; warm pods of a tenant-owned pool carry the owner label from creation.
- A network policy takes effect some time after the stamp: a worst case of 28 ms on Calico and 32.89 s on Cilium in round 10, which is an observation rather than a bound. A dropped SYN looks like a timeout, and an existing socket recovers only on its retransmit, so `netagent` dials a group with a fresh socket, an attempt timeout of at most 1 s, and a retry budget of at least 60 s, and the fetch retry window has to allow for it.
- An assignment applies to pods stamped afterwards, so a tenant's running unstamped pods stay on the default Envoy until they retire. A revoked assignment is refused at the decision at once, so a stamped pod's egress fails closed. Claim and rebind compare the pod's label with the current assignment, and on a mismatch they refuse the pod and arrange its drain, so reusing a reserved pod for a new binding (`reserved → bound`, §4.6.1) never hands a session a pod with dead egress. At rebind the reservation has to end first, as the gateway's hold release (`endReservedHolds`) does, because marking a reserved pod for draining (the `lenny.dev/drain-request` label) would move it from `reserved` to `draining`, a transition the pod state machine (`state.ValidTransitions`) does not allow. Tunnels already open to the old group run to the pool's cap, which is a stated residual.
- A group's address carries trust: a firewall or `aws:SourceIp` rule reads it as the holder. A group is therefore exclusive to one tenant by default. Sharing needs an explicit setting, and each holder is notified and can see it in the egress address read. A released address waits out a cool-off, 30 days by default, before it can go to another tenant.
- The platform admin registers the group's address with the gateway as a location or a trusted proxy, and assigns the group to a tenant.
- A tenant granted a group is tied to it: the binding that `netagent` receives on the pod binding API names the proxy Service to dial, and every CONNECT of that tenant's pods goes to the group's Envoy, whose decisions route some hosts to the corporate gateway and the rest directly, all from the group's address. The group's NetworkPolicy admits only the pods stamped for it once they are bound, and the decision service refuses a pod that dials a group its tenant does not hold.
- `X-Authenticated-User` and `X-Forwarded-For` remain available on the upstream chaining hop for gateways that read them on CONNECT, and for a deployer whose gateway decrypts.

An egress group's address does not cover all of a tenant's traffic. A raw socket from a pod with `egressProfile: internet` leaves from the cluster's shared address, and so does a warm pod of a shared pool before bind. A fetch into the mirror's public store leaves from the mirror's shared route, because that store is filled anonymously through the platform default route. Calls the gateway makes itself, such as an LLM call under `deliveryMode: proxy` or a webhook, leave from the gateway's shared address. A firewall rule on the group's address refuses that traffic, and an abuse block on a shared address still affects the tenant. A per-tenant path for these is out of scope. The operator documentation states this list, so an operator does not promise that every connection uses the address.

Per-tenant identity at the gateway therefore costs one egress address per identity the gateway must distinguish.

Level 2 and level 1 differ in what a component outside the pod can enforce:

- **Level 2** gives per-session policy, revocation at the proxy, and decision-level audit, through a protocol (ext_authz) that Envoy, Squid with a helper, and a Smokescreen-based proxy can all speak.
- **Level 1** gives a host list per identity at the proxy, and everything finer is enforced by `netagent` in the pod. The next section states what that gives up.

Cloud FQDN firewalls identify clients by source address or node identity, so they cannot implement either level. They serve as perimeter controls behind `lenny-egress`.

### Tenant outbound connector

A customer's private network is the most common reason a customer admin needs the operator's help: self-managed git and registries behind a firewall. A tenant outbound connector is a customer-run agent that dials out to the platform, so traffic reaches that network with no inbound firewall rule and no platform registration. It is specified in [`tenant-outbound-connector-design.md`](tenant-outbound-connector-design.md), with its own proposals.

This design provides most of what it needs, and it changes no decision contract. That design runs an in-cluster outbound gateway, which this section calls by that name to keep it apart from Lenny's API gateway.
